# Content Status

The most important file to read at the start of a content session: it records what is written versus scaffolding. **Keep it current** when you add content.

**A new helper, `scratchpad/prune_trims.py`, was added.** `merge_trims.py` aborts the whole batch on the first field that is not strictly shorter — correct, since that is how text pasted back unchanged gets caught — but with a large hand-written trims file it cost a round-trip per no-op field (it fired five times this set). `prune_trims.py` drops only the no-op entries, prints exactly what it dropped, and leaves the genuine trims to go through the unchanged `merge_trims.py` gate. It never edits text.

Earlier: **READING DENSITY SHIPPED (2026-08-09) — Brief · Standard · Detailed toggle in the Tracker, plus the full Neurology content.** Engine in `index.html`, data shape and all content in `data.js`.

**What landed.** (1) A three-stop density control in the Tracker toolbar, synced across devices, with a **per-card pin** so one condition can sit at a different register to the rest. (2) A required **`depth`** key on all **558** entries recording which register the existing text is written at — the audit. (3) Authored **`standard` and `brief`** objects for all **45 Neurology conditions**, whose existing text becomes Detailed.

**The audit result is the important number: 428 of 558 entries are written at BRIEF register, 51 at standard and 79 at detailed.** So the app's existing text is mostly brief, not standard — HFpEF (192 chars/field) is well above the app median of 106. Per specialty, Neurology is the sole outlier at 45/45 detailed; Dermatology, ENT, Haematology, Psychiatry and Public Health are 100% brief. **All 45 Neurology entries were forced to `detailed` by hand rather than by threshold** — `neuro_neurogenic_shock` and `neuro_autonomic_dysreflexia` fall just under a pure character cut-off (259 and 265 chars/field) but plainly belong with the rest, which is exactly why the boundary was reviewed rather than trusted.

**The fallback is what makes the control shippable now.** `resolveDensity` resolves authored → base → derived-shorter → capped-fuller, so every one of the 558 entries responds to the toggle today and simply improves as text is written. A card asked for a register fuller than anything written says **"no fuller version written yet"** rather than pretending; a derived one says **"shortened automatically"**. Full rules in `data-schemas.md`.

**⚠️ Highlight spans are now namespaced `"<level>:<fieldKey>"`.** Highlights are character offsets into field text, so without this a span recorded on the Detailed text would land on the wrong words in Standard. Pre-density spans have bare keys and are surfaced only while that condition's base register is on screen — a lazy migration with no data loss. "Clear N" is scoped to the visible register.

**Registers achieved in Neurology:** brief **110** chars/field over the core four, standard **229** over seven fields (**1,606** per card against HFpEF's 1,345), detailed **480**. Standard runs slightly above HFpEF because every Neurology entry also carries a Complications row, which HFpEF does not.

Verified: balance **0/0/0**; `node --check` passes; **558 conditions, 558 unique ids, 0 duplicate top-level keys** across all 558 objects (bracket-matched, string-aware); all 45 Neurology entries have complete `standard` (8 fields) and `brief` (4 fields) objects with **0 register defects**. **Checked live in the running app**: Multiple sclerosis renders 8 rows of authored text at Standard and 4 at Brief; Acne vulgaris (brief base) correctly reports *"Detailed · no fuller version written yet"*; pinning a card to Brief while the global control stays Detailed shows *"Brief · pinned"* and both persist to `localStorage`. **Re-upload `data.js` AND `index.html`** — the engine change is inert without the latter.

**⏭️ REMAINING, and it is the bulk of the job: 442 of the 509 genuine conditions still have no `complications` field**, which the user wants on every condition at Standard and Detailed. **Guideline positions verified on the web rather than recalled.** DCM: **there is no NICE guideline**, so `source: "AO Spine / CSRS"` — the 2017 AO Spine / Cervical Spine Research Society guideline remains the authoritative one and was reaffirmed by the 2025 AO Spine Clinical Practice Recommendations. The chart is built on the **mJOA score as the treatment-defining variable** (15–17 mild, 12–14 moderate, under 12 severe): moderate and severe get surgical decompression, mild is a shared decision between surgery and a supervised rehabilitation trial with conversion on deterioration. Two points deliberately carried because they are the common errors: **radiological cord compression is present in over 20% of asymptomatic adults**, so MRI must be read against the examination, and **plain radiographs cannot exclude myelopathy and must never be used to reassure**. RP: `source: "NICE HST11 / RCOphth"` — **NICE HST11 (2019) recommends voretigene neparvovec for biallelic RPE65-mediated disease only**, which is the one genuine treatment fork; everything else is supportive. **High-dose vitamin A is explicitly framed as no longer routine** (weak evidence, hepatotoxic, specifically harmful in ABCA4-related disease), which contradicts older teaching, and the **DVLA node is on the chart** because field loss, not acuity, is what fails the standard — patients can read 6/6 and still be unfit to drive.

**⚠️ Guide errors, and this section is the sloppiest so far:**
- **The Perthes disease management text is actually OSGOOD-SCHLATTER'S** — "protective pad over the tibial tubercle", quadriceps and hamstring stretching, "activity continuation". That is a copy-paste error: Perthes management is containment of the femoral head with physiotherapy, bracing and, in older children, osteotomy. The notebook covers Perthes in `paediatric_hip_conditions` and Osgood-Schlatter here, correctly attributed.
- **The PACES boxes are attached to the wrong conditions throughout**: the box under *Cerebral Palsy* describes AUTISM, and the box under *Epilepsy* describes cerebral palsy. Layout, not content, but it would mislead anyone revising straight from the page.
- **JIA analgesia lists "weak opioids e.g. codeine"** — **codeine is contraindicated under 12 years** (MHRA), so it cannot be the weak-opioid step in a paediatric rheumatology ladder. This is the second time this session the guide has recommended a codeine or morphine step that is wrong; the notebook states the correct position.
- **ADHD: "all drugs are cardiotoxic, so baseline ECG should be conducted"** overstates NICE NG87, which requires a baseline ECG before starting **only where there is a personal or family history of cardiac disease, sudden death, or an abnormal cardiovascular examination** — not universally. Baseline height, weight, pulse and blood pressure ARE universal, and the monitoring schedule (height 6-monthly, weight 3-monthly, pulse and BP 6-monthly) is carried in full.
- JIA "inflammatory cytokine blockade e.g. TNF-alpha inhibitors, interleukin receptor antagonists, **anti-emetics**" — the last item is garbled and was dropped.

**Content decisions:**
- **`cerebral_palsy`** is written around the NG62 framing: the lesion is static but its manifestations change; the **red flags for a progressive disorder** (loss of acquired skills, no risk factors, family history, unexpected focal signs) that mean the diagnosis is wrong; GMFCS as the functional grading that actually predicts need; and — given its own blockquote — **ask about PAIN at every review**, since it affects most patients and is the commonest cause of distressed behaviour. Hip surveillance by GMFCS level is included, and the point that **communication difficulty does not equate to learning disability**.
- **`paediatric_jia`** leads with the subtype table and gives **asymptomatic chronic anterior uveitis** its own blockquote, because slit-lamp screening is scheduled rather than symptom-driven, plus **macrophage activation syndrome** as the systemic-JIA emergency with its falling ESR and rising ferritin.
- **`paediatric_fractures`** pairs children's fracture patterns (greenstick, buckle, plastic deformation, Salter-Harris with the SALTR mnemonic) with the **non-accidental injury** assessment, and states the differential — osteogenesis imperfecta, rickets, copper deficiency, prematurity — explicitly, because a wrong accusation is itself a serious harm.
- **`paediatric_seizure_disorders`** carries the numbers parents actually ask for: **recurrence about a third, later epilepsy 1 to 2% after a simple febrile convulsion**, no brain damage, and the 5-minute rule for calling an ambulance. It ends with the funny-turn differential and the reminder to **ECG any exertional or emotional syncope**.
- **`muscular_dystrophies`** contrasts Duchenne, Becker (where **cardiomyopathy can be disproportionate to the weakness**) and myotonic dystrophy (anticipation, maternal congenital form, **annual ECG for conduction block**, anaesthetic sensitivity), with spinal muscular atrophy flagged as the other floppy-infant diagnosis.

**⚠️ THE MOST IMPORTANT CORRECTION IN THIS BATCH IS A DRUG SAFETY ONE, AND THE GUIDE HAS IT BACKWARDS.** For sickle cell crisis the guide says *"oral and IV analgesia (avoid morphine < 12 years)"*. That is wrong and, if followed, would leave a child in severe pain untreated. **Morphine is the standard strong opioid for severe sickle crisis in children.** The drug contraindicated under 12 is **CODEINE**, because unpredictable CYP2D6 ultra-rapid metabolism converts it to morphine at dangerous rates — with a specific MHRA warning after tonsillectomy or adenoidectomy in children with obstructive sleep apnoea. The notebook states the correction explicitly rather than silently, because the guide's version is memorable and would otherwise stick. Web-verified against MHRA and paediatric sickle guidance.

**The other guideline change: UTI imaging.** The guide reproduces the old CG54 approach ("follow up with ultrasound, MCUG and DMSA"). **NICE NG224 (2022) replaced it** and is much less interventionist: **do not routinely image to localise a UTI**; use power Doppler ultrasound (or DMSA if unavailable) only where confirming upper tract involvement genuinely matters; use **DMSA 4 to 6 months after the acute infection** to detect scarring; and reserve micturating cystourethrography for selected infants. The topic is written to NG224, with the atypical-UTI features that actually drive investigation.

**31 guide conditions were compressed into 8 topics, because most of the haematology is already owned.** The existing `haematology` section (13 topics) covers anaemia, haemoglobinopathies, bleeding disorders, leukaemia, lymphoma and thrombocytopenia in adult depth, and `renal` owns glomerulonephritis and stones. Rather than duplicate them, this batch carries **only the paediatric-specific material with cross-references**: newborn screening, penicillin and folate prophylaxis, transcranial Doppler from age 2, hydroxycarbamide thresholds and the transfusion-chelation problem for haemoglobinopathy; the ITP-versus-leukaemia distinction and the paediatric bleeding pattern table for bruising; and childhood tumour recognition rather than oncological staging.

**Content decisions worth recording:**
- **`paediatric_nephrotic_hus`** pairs the two conditions because both are paediatric-specific and both invert adult practice. The nephrotic entry leads with the point that **over 80% is minimal change disease and steroid-responsive, so no biopsy is needed before treatment** — the reverse of adult practice — with the 60 mg/m² regimen and the features that mandate biopsy. The HUS entry carries the counter-intuitive rule that **antibiotics, antimotility agents, opioids and NSAIDs are AVOIDED in diarrhoea-associated HUS** because antibiotics increase Shiga toxin release, and that hypertension is treated with a calcium channel blocker rather than an ACE inhibitor.
- **`paediatric_aki_ckd`** is written around what differs in children: **growth hormone resistance** treated with recombinant GH, renal osteodystrophy, gastrostomy feeding for growth, and **salt and water supplementation** — the opposite of adult restriction.
- **`paediatric_enuresis`** adds the gap the guide leaves: **constipation is the commonest missed cause** of both daytime and night-time wetting, and treating it alone resolves a substantial proportion.
- **`paediatric_scrotum_genitalia`** leads with a three-way table separating torsion, torsion of the appendix testis and epididymo-orchitis, and states plainly that **the diagnosis of torsion is surgical, not radiological** — do not delay exploration for an ultrasound. Also carries the rule that **a boy with hypospadias must not be circumcised before repair**, and the 24-hour referral trigger for bilateral undescended testes or ambiguous genitalia.
- **`paediatric_oncology`** adds the referral red flags the guide omits entirely (the guide gives only treatment), and the **midline discriminator** between the two abdominal masses: **neuroblastoma crosses the midline, Wilms tumour does not**.
- **`paediatric_bruising_bleeding`** puts the safeguarding assessment first — **bruising in a child who is not yet independently mobile** — alongside the medical differential, rather than treating them as alternatives.

**How the 20-odd guide conditions were grouped into 8 topics.** The guide lists them alphabetically, which teaches nothing, so they were regrouped by clinical problem: inflammatory disease (eczema, acne), the nappy area and infant scalp (nappy rash with seborrhoeic dermatitis, since the diagnostic question is the same), bacterial infection (cellulitis, periorbital cellulitis, necrotising fasciitis), infestations (scabies, head lice, ringworm), viral lesions (molluscum, warts, hand foot and mouth), birthmarks, and a final recognition topic carrying the guide's DermNet rash table.

**Content decisions and the points the guide leaves out:**
- **Eczema** carries the full severity table with the matching steroid potency, and the rule that a failed steroid should be swapped for **a different preparation of the same potency before escalating** — most apparent failures are under-use from steroid phobia. **Eczema herpeticum** is set out as the emergency, with the punched-out-erosion description parents should be taught.
- **Acne** states the co-prescribing rule the guide implies but does not emphasise — **never an oral antibiotic without a topical retinoid or benzoyl peroxide** — plus the tetracycline age limit of 12 years, and the isotretinoin **Pregnancy Prevention Programme** and MHRA mental-health advice, neither of which the guide mentions.
- **Nappy rash** gains the discriminating table the guide lacks (**irritant SPARES the flexures, candida INVOLVES them with satellite pustules**) and the sequencing point that hydrocortisone goes on first and the barrier preparation a few minutes later. Cradle cap is paired with it and given its own discriminator: **it does not itch**.
- **Periorbital cellulitis** is written around the features that separate it from **orbital** cellulitis — painful or restricted eye movements, proptosis, reduced acuity, colour desaturation, relative afferent pupillary defect — which the guide does not list at all despite it being the reason the condition matters.
- **Infestations** carries the practical detail that gets treatment wrong: the **second permethrin application at 10 to 14 days**, treating the whole household simultaneously, that **itch persists 2 to 4 weeks after cure** (the commonest cause of unnecessary re-treatment), that **infants need face and scalp included**, and that **tinea capitis always needs systemic antifungal** because topicals do not reach the follicle.
- **Birthmarks** gap-fills what the guide omits entirely: **port-wine stain versus haemangioma** (present at birth and permanent versus appearing later and involuting), Sturge-Weber, erythema toxicum, miliaria and salmon patch. **Congenital dermal melanocytosis** keeps the guide's safeguarding point — it is mistaken for bruising, so record it at birth.
- **The rash approach** topic leads with **the non-blanching rule**, then the other must-not-miss rashes, then a 12-row recognition table, then guttate psoriasis, HSP and bites. The HSP entry adds the follow-up the guide omits: **blood pressure and urinalysis for at least 6 months**, because the nephritis appears late and silently.

**The user was asked which cluster tuberous sclerosis should go in, and AGAIN chose a new cluster over the recommendation.** The options were *Single-gene disorders* (recommended — autosomal dominant, and it would sit next to NF1 and NF2), *Hereditary cancer syndromes* (filing it by the lifelong tumour surveillance that actually drives management), or a new **Neurocutaneous syndromes** cluster. The user took the third. **This is the fourth new cluster created this way** (after Neurology's *Spinal cord & autonomic disorders* and *Raised ICP & hydrocephalus*, and Gastro's *Neuroendocrine tumours*), and the pattern is now unmistakable: when a condition sits at the intersection of two existing clusters, this user prefers a new, clinically coherent grouping over forcing it into either. **Recommend accordingly in future — lead with the new-cluster option where one is genuinely defensible.**

**Cluster placement and the NF1 move.** *Neurocutaneous syndromes* was inserted **between *Single-gene disorders* and *Hereditary cancer syndromes***, which is the natural bridge — these are single-gene conditions whose whole management is tumour surveillance. `genetics_nf1` was moved out of *Single-gene disorders* (which drops 8 → 7) by a script that rewrites **only the cluster string** and asserts the line's length changes by exactly the difference between the two cluster names, so no field text could have been altered. Every genetics cluster is confirmed contiguous afterwards.

**`genetics_nf2` — 16-node flowchart, `source: "NF2 specialist service"`.** Named **"Neurofibromatosis type 2 (NF2-related schwannomatosis)"** so it is findable under both the old and the 2022 consensus name — the same convention used for the vestibular schwannoma entry. The entry is built on the point that **NF2 is not a more severe NF1 but a different disease**: bilateral vestibular schwannomas, meningiomas and ependymomas from loss of **merlin** on 22q12, presenting in the late teens to 30s with bilateral hearing loss, and with **no Lisch nodules, no axillary freckling and no optic glioma**. Two management principles carried: **the aim is preserving function, above all hearing, not removing every tumour**, with **bevacizumab** able to shrink schwannomas and improve hearing; and **hearing rehabilitation is planned in advance** — cochlear implant if the cochlear nerve is intact, auditory brainstem implant if not, with lip-reading and sign language taught BEFORE hearing is lost. Also captured: about half of cases are de novo and many are **mosaic**, so a negative blood test does not exclude it and tumour tissue should be tested.

**`genetics_tuberous_sclerosis` — 16-node flowchart, `source: "TSC international consensus"`.** Organised around the mechanism, because it explains the treatment: the **hamartin-tuberin complex normally inhibits mTORC1**, so losing TSC1 or TSC2 leaves mTOR signalling unchecked — which is why **everolimus and sirolimus shrink lesions in brain, kidney, lung and skin alike** and are the closest thing to disease-modifying therapy. High-yield points carried: the **Vogt triad occurs together in only about a third**; **Wood's lamp** examination for ash-leaf macules, plus shagreen patch, angiofibromas and Koenen tumours; **vigabatrin is first-line for infantile spasms specifically in tuberous sclerosis**, with irreversible visual field constriction as the trade-off; **renal angiomyolipoma haemorrhage and chronic kidney disease are the commonest cause of death in adults**, so everolimus or embolisation rather than nephrectomy; **lymphangioleiomyomatosis** in adult women; the **TSC2-PKD1 contiguous deletion** giving tuberous sclerosis with polycystic kidneys; and **TSC-associated neuropsychiatric disorders in around 90%**, which cause more day-to-day difficulty than the tumours and are the most under-treated aspect.

**Flagged, not done:** `genetics_nf1` is still at the old thin standard (no complications, no flowchart) and now sits between two full-depth entries in its new cluster, which looks inconsistent. Upgrading it was outside what was asked. The same is true of most of the remaining Genetics section — only `genetics_huntingtons`, `genetics_nf2` and `genetics_tuberous_sclerosis` carry the current seven-field depth with complications and a chart. **Genetics is the obvious candidate for the next depth audit**, on the model of the neurology one.

**Duplicate check clean this time** — the only prior hits for "extradural" or "epidural" were inside `neuro_cord_compression`, `neuro_cauda_equina` and `neuro_neurogenic_shock`, where spinal epidural abscess and haematoma appear as causes of cord compression. That is a different entity and correctly stays where it is. (Five of the last six addition requests turned out to be duplicates or near-duplicates, so the check is now routine.)

**15-node flowchart, `source: "NICE NG232"`.** Built around the two things that decide outcome. **The CT shape is the diagnosis** — biconvex "lemon", limited by the cranial sutures because the dura is bound there, but able to cross the midline, which is the exact inverse of the crescentic subdural that crosses sutures but is stopped by the falx. And **the lucid interval is a window, not reassurance**: arterial blood has to strip the adherent dura from the inner table, which takes force and time, so the initial concussion appears to recover while the clot enlarges. The entry states plainly that outcome is excellent when the clot is evacuated **before the pupil dilates** and poor once herniation has begun, because the underlying brain is often uninjured — which is what makes this the intracranial haematoma with the best prognosis and the one where delay is least forgivable.

Other content carried: the **NG232 CT-within-1-hour criteria** and the separate anticoagulant rule (CT within 8 hours, or within 1 hour if presenting more than 8 hours after injury); middle meningeal artery in about three-quarters, with venous extradural haematomas at the vertex and posterior fossa behaving more slowly; the **swirl sign** of active bleeding; rarity under 2 and over 60 because the dura is adherent at those ages; the herniation sequence with **ipsilateral fixed dilated pupil and contralateral hemiparesis**, plus **Kernohan's notch** as the false localising exception; conservative management only in a neurosurgical unit with hourly observations and planned repeat CT; and **no corticosteroids, per CRASH**.

**Guide errors corrected silently, per the standing convention:**
- **"Viral: HSV 2 (most common cause)" for encephalitis is WRONG** — **HSV-1** causes the great majority of sporadic herpes encephalitis; HSV-2 causes neonatal disease and, in adults, a benign recurrent meningitis. The existing `encephalitis` topic already states type 1 correctly, so nothing needed changing — but the guide's claim is recorded here because it is a confident, memorable error.
- **"Elderly: Group B Strep, Listeria monocytogenes"** — misleading. In older adults the commonest organisms remain **Streptococcus pneumoniae and Neisseria meningitidis**; Listeria is the additional organism that drives the addition of amoxicillin, and Group B Streptococcus is a neonatal pathogen.
- **"IV ceftriaxone and corticosteroids (unless meningococcal)"** is too crude to act on. The notebook gives the actual rule: **dexamethasone 0.15 mg/kg (max 10 mg) 6-hourly for 4 days, with or just before the first antibiotic dose**, withheld in meningococcal septicaemia, and — if already delayed more than 12 hours after antibiotics — only after specialist advice.
- Minor: "acyclovir" (UK spelling aciclovir), "Western Nile Virus", and a blank appearance cell in the fungal CSF row.

**Web-verified for this batch:** NG240 requires **intravenous antibiotics within 1 hour of arrival**; **audiological assessment within 4 weeks** of bacterial meningitis, since sensorineural hearing loss is the commonest sequela; and close contacts of meningococcal disease need **a single dose of ciprofloxacin** arranged through public health. Carried from earlier verification: the **2024 McDonald revisions** for multiple sclerosis, and the ESCMID 2024 brain abscess points (diffusion-weighted MRI, and withholding antimicrobials until aspiration where it is safe to wait).

**Content decisions.** `spinal_cord_syndromes` deliberately merges the two emergencies (metastatic cord compression and cauda equina, with a side-by-side table), the five anatomical syndromes, and the injury complications (spinal shock, neurogenic shock, autonomic dysreflexia) into one topic, because they are only distinguishable from each other and read poorly apart. `cranial_nerve_lesions` starts with the forehead question, since that single discrimination separates a stroke from Bell's palsy, and then covers third and sixth nerve palsies and Horner syndrome — with the two rules that matter, the **pupil deciding urgency in a third nerve palsy** and a **painful Horner syndrome being carotid dissection until proved otherwise**. `peripheral_neuropathy` is organised around pattern rather than aetiology, so that **mononeuritis multiplex is flagged as meaning vasculitis until proved otherwise**.

The roadmap's remaining rows are the paediatrics thread (16f to 16i), psychiatry (19), and the two orphan Path Guide blocks (17 and 18).

**Verified on the web before writing, and three items had moved:**
- **DVLA rules are more granular than usually taught.** After a FIRST unprovoked seizure the requirement is **6 months** off driving, extended to **12 months** only where investigations or clinical factors suggest higher recurrence risk. Established epilepsy needs **12 months seizure-free**, and after **5 seizure-free years** a licence to age 70 is usually issued. Group 2 requires 10 years seizure-free off medication.
- **Cenobamate** is recommended by NICE (TA753) as a **second-line add-on** in drug-resistant focal epilepsy, after at least two antiseizure medicines and at least one first-line add-on.
- **Medication-overuse headache withdrawal is ABRUPT, not tapered** — NICE CG150 advises stopping the overused drug **all at once for at least one month**, with an explicit warning that the headache worsens for one to two weeks first. Patients who are not warned restart the drug and conclude withdrawal does not work.

Also carried from earlier verification this session: the **MHRA topiramate Pregnancy Prevention Programme**; rimegepant, atogepant and the CGRP antibodies after three failed preventives; the **MHRA January 2024 valproate restriction** (no new starts under 55, male or female, without two specialists documenting no alternative); and **ESETT** showing levetiracetam, phenytoin and valproate equally effective as second-line agents in status.

**Content decisions worth recording.** `cluster_headache_tacs` covers the trigeminal autonomic cephalalgias as a family rather than cluster headache alone, because the group is separated almost entirely by attack duration and frequency — and because **paroxysmal hemicrania is defined by its absolute response to indometacin**, so a therapeutic trial belongs in any short-lasting unilateral headache with autonomic features. `epilepsy_classification` carries a **seizure versus syncope versus dissociative attack** table, since convulsive syncope is regularly misdiagnosed as epilepsy — which is also why the **12-lead ECG in every first seizure** is stated as mandatory. The epilepsy material was deliberately split into classification and management, because the classification drives the drug choice and the two are long enough to be unwieldy together.

Caught by an odd-single-asterisk count per topic before deployment and reworded to **HLA-B 15:02**. A whole-notebook scan confirms **no other topic has an unbalanced single asterisk**. The three traps are now: no ordered `1.` lists, no indented `  - ` bullets, and **no literal `*` in gene, allele or footnote notation**.

**⚠️ A correction to the previous entry: Huntington's disease was NOT missing from the Tracker.** The Batch 21 write-up flagged both Huntington's and essential tremor as absent, but that flag was compiled by searching only the `neurology` section — `genetics_huntingtons` has existed all along in **Genetics → *Single-gene disorders***, beside Marfan, NF1, cystic fibrosis, fragile X and Duchenne. **Only essential tremor was genuinely new.** The lesson is the same one the meningitis request taught: search ALL specialties, not the one the condition is being requested for. It was put to the user, who chose to **leave it in Genetics and upgrade it in place** rather than move it or duplicate it — the right call, since Genetics is organised by inheritance pattern and Huntington's is its clearest example of an autosomal dominant trinucleotide-repeat disease with anticipation.

**`genetics_huntingtons` upgraded in place** from a thin entry (fields of 46 to 121 characters, no complications, no flowchart) to current standard: **fields of 296 to 936 characters, a Complications row, and a 17-node flowchart** with `source: "UK Huntington's Prediction Consortium"`. The chart is built around the split that matters — **a symptomatic patient gets a diagnostic test, an asymptomatic relative enters the formal PREDICTIVE testing protocol through clinical genetics, never a routine blood test** — and then branches on the **CAG repeat result**: 40 or more fully penetrant, 36 to 39 reduced penetrance, 27 to 35 an intermediate allele that causes no disease but may EXPAND in offspring. Also carried: **asymptomatic children are not tested**; anticipation is far greater in **paternal** transmission, which is why juvenile-onset (Westphal) disease is paternally inherited; a negative family history does not exclude it, because relatives are often labelled with dementia, alcoholism or psychiatric illness; **tetrabenazine can precipitate depression and must be preceded by suicide risk screening** in a population whose suicide risk is already several times baseline; and chorea the patient has not noticed should not be treated.

**`neuro_essential_tremor` — 16-node flowchart, `source: "NICE CKS"`.** Inserted after `neuro_msa`, so the cluster now reads Parkinson's → PSP → MSA → **Essential tremor** → MND → Friedreich's, which keeps the parkinsonism-and-its-mimics group together before the hyperkinetic and ataxic entries. Built around the discrimination that the whole diagnosis turns on: **essential tremor is postural and action tremor that is ABSENT at rest with no bradykinesia**, bilateral and roughly symmetrical, often with head or voice involvement, and characteristically suppressed by a small amount of alcohol — while parkinsonian tremor is the reverse. Handwriting is a neat discriminator: large and tremulous here, micrographic in Parkinson's disease. The chart forces two checks before the label is applied — **review the drug chart and thyroid function**, since thyrotoxicosis, salbutamol, theophylline, lithium, valproate, SSRIs and caffeine all produce a reversible enhanced physiological tremor, and **exclude Wilson's disease under 40**. Treatment is driven by functional impact rather than visibility: **propranolol first-line**, **primidone** the equally effective alternative started at 25 to 50 mg at night with an explicit warning about the first-dose reaction (the commonest reason patients abandon an effective drug), then topiramate or gabapentin, botulinum toxin for isolated head or voice tremor, and referral for **deep brain stimulation of the VIM thalamic nucleus or MRI-guided focused ultrasound thalamotomy** in severe refractory disease.

**Two topics go beyond the guide because the guide has no framework for them.** `neurodegeneration_proteins` takes the guide's four-row misfolded-protein table and rebuilds it as the organising principle of the whole batch — tau, beta-amyloid, alpha-synuclein, **TDP-43**, **polyglutamine** and **prion protein** — with a section explaining why the clinical syndromes overlap (Parkinson's and dementia with Lewy bodies as near-identical synucleinopathies; motor neurone disease and frontotemporal dementia sharing TDP-43 and C9orf72; progressive supranuclear palsy and corticobasal degeneration both tauopathies). `parkinsonism_and_tremor` adds the differential the guide never states: the red flags against Parkinson's disease, a three-way comparison of the Parkinson-plus syndromes, the two reversible causes (**drug-induced** and **vascular** parkinsonism), and a **tremor table** separating parkinsonian from essential from physiological tremor — high-yield for the exam and absent from the source entirely.

**Guide errors corrected silently in the notebook, per the standing convention:**
- **"Frontotemporal dementia a.k.a. Pick's disease" is wrong.** Pick's disease is one histological subtype defined by tau-positive Pick bodies, and accounts for only a minority of frontotemporal dementia. The notebook states this explicitly because the equation is so commonly taught.
- **"Mutations in alpha-synuclein are responsible for PD"** — misleading. SNCA mutations cause only rare familial disease; the overwhelming majority of Parkinson's disease is sporadic, with LRRK2 and GBA the commoner genetic contributors.
- **The guide lists dementia with Lewy bodies under "Parkinson plus syndromes"**, which conflates a dementia with the atypical parkinsonian syndromes. The notebook keeps Parkinson-plus for progressive supranuclear palsy, multiple system atrophy and corticobasal degeneration, and handles Lewy body disease with the dementias, noting the one-year rule.
- **Frontotemporal dementia genetics given as "progranulin gene" only** — **C9orf72** is the commonest genetic cause and is the link to motor neurone disease; MAPT and GRN account for most of the rest.
- **Alzheimer's management given as "anti-cholinesterases, nAChR agonists, glutamate antagonists"** — nicotinic agonists are not a treatment class in practice. Replaced with the NG97 ladder (donepezil, galantamine, rivastigmine, then memantine) plus the electrocardiogram caution.
- **Vascular dementia described only as "infarcts of small and medium sized vessels"** — subcortical small-vessel disease with lacunes and confluent white matter change is the commonest mechanism and explains the early gait disturbance and incontinence.
- **"Iatrogenic CJD (following flood transfusions of surgical procedures)"** — a typo for blood transfusion or surgical procedures; also the guide's Creutzfeldt-Jakob spelling is wrong in one place.
- The misfolded-protein table **omits TDP-43, prion protein and polyglutamine** entirely.

**⚠️ Two things web-verified this session that the guide predates:**
- **RT-QuIC is now the key CSF test for sporadic CJD** — real-time quaking-induced conversion amplifies misfolded prion protein with sensitivity above 90% and specificity approaching 100%, and it has been part of the diagnostic criteria **since 2017**. The guide gives only 14-3-3 and S100, which are far less specific and simply reflect rapid neuronal destruction.
- **Lecanemab and donanemab are MHRA-licensed but NOT available on the NHS** — NICE rejected both in final guidance in 2025, for the third time, as too small a benefit for the cost.
- Also carried: the **Huntington's CAG thresholds** (under 27 normal, 27 to 35 intermediate with germline instability, 36 to 39 reduced penetrance, 40 or more fully penetrant), paternal anticipation, and the ethics of predictive testing including that asymptomatic children are not tested.

**Gaps flagged, not filled:** **Huntington's disease and essential tremor are NOT Tracker conditions**, despite now having notebook coverage — Huntington's is a major autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disease and essential tremor is the commonest movement disorder in primary care. Both are strong candidates for the *Movement disorders & neurodegeneration* cluster if the user wants them. Corticobasal degeneration is likewise notebook-only.

**`neuro_brain_abscess` — 17-node flowchart, `source: "ESCMID 2024"`.** Built on the 2024 ESCMID guideline, web-verified rather than recalled. The two points the chart is constructed around: **MRI with DIFFUSION-WEIGHTED imaging is what separates abscess from necrotic tumour** — pus is viscous and cellular so diffusion is RESTRICTED with a low ADC, whereas a tumour's necrotic centre facilitates it — and **ESCMID conditionally recommends WITHHOLDING antimicrobials until aspiration** where the patient is stable enough to wait, because prior antibiotics substantially reduce culture yield. Also carried: the classic triad is present in **fewer than a quarter** of patients and **fever is absent in about half**; **lumbar puncture is contraindicated**; the capsule is **thinner on its ventricular side**, which is why rupture is into the ventricle and why that complication is catastrophic; aspiration for lesions of about **2.5 cm** or more; empirical **ceftriaxone plus metronidazole**, adding vancomycin after trauma or neurosurgery; **6 to 8 weeks** of treatment with oral step-down in selected patients; **dexamethasone only for significant oedema, stopped as soon as possible because it reduces antibiotic penetration**; and the site-by-source rule (otogenic → temporal lobe and cerebellum, sinusitic → frontal lobe, haematogenous → multiple, at the grey-white junction).

**Naming note:** the user chose *CNS tumours* / *CNS infections*, which means Neurology's *CNS infections* now sits alongside Infectious Diseases' *CNS infection* (singular). That is intentional and was flagged at the time; the alternative offered was *CNS infection & inflammation*, which would also have left room for autoimmune encephalitis, neurosarcoidosis and cerebral vasculitis later.

It was rewritten to current depth with complications and a 15-node flowchart.

**⚠️ THE BIGGEST GUIDELINE CHANGE FOUND, and it reverses standard teaching: dopamine agonists are NO LONGER first-line for restless legs syndrome.** Because **7 to 10% of patients a year develop AUGMENTATION**, both **NICE CKS (updated 2025)** and the **2025 American Academy of Sleep Medicine guideline** now put **alpha-2-delta ligands (gabapentin enacarbil first, then gabapentin or pregabalin) and correction of iron deficiency (ferritin target above 75 micrograms/L) FIRST**, with a conditional recommendation *against* routine pramipexole and ropinirole — drugs the 2012 AASM guideline recommended strongly. The entry carries this explicitly, alongside impulse control disorders (up to 1 in 6, warn the patient AND a family member), sudden onset of sleep, dopamine agonist withdrawal syndrome, and the fibrotic risks of the ergot-derived agents.

**Other guideline drift corrected in the flowcharts (all web-verified this session):**
- **Epilepsy:** the chart predated the **MHRA January 2024 valproate restriction** — valproate must not be STARTED in anyone under 55, **male or female**, unless two specialists independently document there is no alternative. Added, along with the rule that carbamazepine, oxcarbazepine, phenytoin, gabapentin and pregabalin **exacerbate** absence and myoclonic seizures.
- **Metastatic spinal cord compression:** the old chart said "flat bed rest + spinal precautions". **NICE NG234 (2023) moved away from routine flat bed rest** — keep the person mobile where the spine is stable, nurse supine only where immobilisation is genuinely needed, and adjust position for pain or breathlessness. Also added: dexamethasone 16 mg continued daily while awaiting treatment, the caution about steroids before biopsy in suspected lymphoma, and **radiotherapy within 24 hours** where surgery is not appropriate.
- **Stroke:** the old chart gave thrombectomy as "6–24 h" without the 6-hour window or the imaging criteria, and put aspirin "after 24 h" without distinguishing the post-thrombolysis delay. Rebuilt to NG128 with tenecteplase, the three thrombectomy windows, the BP rules and decompressive hemicraniectomy.
- **Subarachnoid haemorrhage:** the old chart went straight to "LP at 12 h" if CT was negative. Rebuilt around the **NG228 6-hour rule** — a normal CT within 6 hours effectively excludes SAH and needs no LP.
- **Migraine:** rebuilt for the **topiramate Pregnancy Prevention Programme**, rimegepant, atogepant and the CGRP antibodies after three failed preventives.
- **Alzheimer's:** records that **lecanemab and donanemab are MHRA-licensed but were rejected by NICE for NHS use in final guidance (June 2025)** — their third rejection.
- **Multiple sclerosis:** new chart, built on the **2024 McDonald revisions** (optic nerve as a fifth topography, central vein sign, paramagnetic rim lesions, kappa free light chains, diagnosis possible at first presentation).
- **Motor neurone disease:** **tofersen** for SOD1-associated disease was MHRA-approved in July 2025, with NHS funding still being determined.

**Flowcharts: 12 new, 13 rebuilt.** New charts for cluster headache, trigeminal neuralgia, IIH, subdural haematoma, MND, MS, CNS tumours, encephalitis, Wernicke's, plus the three new conditions. Rebuilt: migraine, stroke, TIA, SAH, epilepsy, status epilepticus, Parkinson's, dementia, myasthenia, GBS, cord compression, cauda equina, Bell's palsy. **Every rebuild fixed at least one unlabelled fork** — the old charts routinely had a decision node with only a "yes" branch, or a plain node with one labelled and one unlabelled edge.

**⚠️ Process bug worth recording: one condition stored its `management` object AFTER `cluster:` in JSON-quoted-key style (`management: {"def": …}`), unlike every other entry.** The update script split each object at `, cluster: "` and looked for `management: {` only in the head, so for `neuro_status_epilepticus` it found none and inserted a second one after the id — leaving a **duplicate key**, with the stale trailing object silently winning at parse time. Caught by a duplicate-key audit over every condition object (only that one was affected) and fixed by deleting the stale copy. **Future edit scripts must scan the WHOLE object for a key, not just the part before `cluster:`, and must assert the key count is 1 afterwards.**

Eight new notebook topics plus a full rewrite of `stroke_tia`.** Notebook `neurology` **5 → 13 topics**, **total 212 → 220**. **Ordering.** The nine sit as one contiguous cerebrovascular block after `headache_red_flags` and before `cns_tumours`: stroke and TIA → localisation → secondary prevention → the three haemorrhages → traumatic brain injury → raised ICP → hydrocephalus. Raised ICP and hydrocephalus deliberately sit immediately before `cns_tumours`, which is a cause of both.

**⚠️ The most important correction in this batch — the guide has COMMUNICATING and NON-COMMUNICATING HYDROCEPHALUS THE WRONG WAY ROUND (p247).** It defines communicating as "obstruction in outflow of CSF" and gives a neonatal aqueduct example under it, then defines non-communicating as "reduced absorption of CSF into sinus veins" with a post-meningitis example. Both labels and both examples are swapped: **non-communicating (obstructive) means a block *within* the ventricular system — aqueduct stenosis is the textbook case — while communicating means CSF reaches the subarachnoid space but is not absorbed, as after meningitis or subarachnoid haemorrhage.** This is not a pedantic point: **lumbar puncture is absolutely contraindicated in obstructive hydrocephalus but is the diagnostic test in normal pressure hydrocephalus**, so learning it backwards is dangerous. The notebook states it correctly and makes the lumbar-puncture consequence an explicit row of the comparison table. (The guide's neonatal sentence — "the lateral ventricles obstruct the cerebral aqueduct" — is also garbled; it is the aqueduct that obstructs.)

**Other guide errors and drift, all corrected silently in the notebook per the standing convention:**
- **Thrombolysis "if <3h from event"** → **4.5 hours**, and the guide has **no thrombectomy at all**. Added: within 6 hours for proximal anterior circulation occlusion, **6 to 24 hours** with salvageable tissue on CT perfusion or diffusion-weighted MRI, and **basilar/posterior cerebral up to 24 hours**; selection at pre-stroke modified Rankin below 3 and NIHSS above 5. **Tenecteplase** as an alternative to alteplase (NICE TA990, 2024) is newer than the guide.
- **"Aspirin +/- dipyridamole"** for both stroke and TIA → **aspirin 300 mg for 2 weeks then clopidogrel 75 mg**, with aspirin plus modified-release dipyridamole demoted to second line, and the **21-day dual antiplatelet** window added (see below).
- **The time-based TIA definition ("<24 hrs")** → the modern **tissue-based** definition: focal ischaemia **without infarction on imaging**. The 24-hour rule is recorded as superseded.
- **Charcot-Bouchard causality is inverted.** The guide says a hypertensive bleed "can cause Charcot-Bouchard microaneurysms". It is the other way round — chronic hypertension produces the microaneurysms, and their rupture produces the bleed.
- **SAH aneurysm site: "most at internal carotid bifurcation"** → the **anterior communicating artery is commonest**, then posterior communicating and the middle cerebral bifurcation. Its "usually <50yrs" is also low; the peak is **45 to 70**.
- **Cerebral amyloid angiopathy is absent from the guide entirely**, despite being the classic cause of **lobar** haemorrhage in older people — gap-filled, along with the deep-versus-lobar split that drives the differential.
- **Herniation anatomy:** "subfalcine — herniation of **singular** cortex" is the **cingulate gyrus**, and the tentorium separates the **occipital lobes** from the cerebellum, not the "parietal lobes". The guide also omits the **third nerve palsy with fixed dilated pupil** of uncal herniation, **Kernohan's notch**, and the **false localising sixth nerve palsy** — all added. "Cytotoxin oedema" is **cytotoxic**.
- **Whole management blocks the guide simply does not have**, now written: nimodipine in SAH; blood-pressure lowering and anticoagulant reversal in intracerebral haemorrhage; the NASCET threshold for carotid endarterectomy; decompressive hemicraniectomy.

**Web-verified, and several of these have moved since the guide was written.** NG128: aspirin **300 mg daily for 2 weeks** after ischaemic stroke; **do not lower blood pressure** unless a hypertensive emergency or pre-thrombolysis (**≤185/110**); in **intracerebral haemorrhage**, presenting within 6 hours with systolic **150–220**, aim for systolic **130–140 within one hour** and avoid a fall greater than 60 mmHg; **statins are not started immediately**; in atrial fibrillation, **aspirin 300 mg for 2 weeks then anticoagulate**; **decompressive hemicraniectomy within 48 hours** (NIHSS >15, ≥50% of the middle cerebral artery territory or >145 cm³); carotid endarterectomy at **50–99% NASCET (70–99% ECST)**, referred **within 1 week** and operated **within 2**; and — a genuine trap — **"do not use scoring systems such as ABCD2"**, which many textbooks still teach. The **2023 National Clinical Guideline for Stroke for the UK and Ireland** supplies the **21-day dual antiplatelet** regimen (clopidogrel 300 then 75 mg plus aspirin 300 then 75 mg within 24 hours of minor stroke or high-risk TIA, then clopidogrel alone; ticagrelor plus aspirin for 30 days as the alternative), which sits alongside rather than inside NG128 — the notebook attributes it explicitly. **NG228**: CT accuracy is **highest within 6 hours** and a normal CT in that window means **no routine lumbar puncture**; after 6 hours, LP **at least 12 hours** from onset, diagnosed on **xanthochromia by spectrophotometry**; CT angiography without delay; **secure the aneurysm at the earliest opportunity because rebleeding risk is highest within 24 hours**, coiling first-line; **enteral nimodipine** (60 mg 4-hourly for 21 days); **euvolaemia and a vasopressor** for delayed cerebral ischaemia; **relatives are not routinely screened**. **NG232** head injury supplies the CT-within-1-hour list and the **anticoagulant/antiplatelet rule — consider CT within 8 hours, or within 1 hour if they present more than 8 hours after the injury**. Two trials are carried as explicit safety points: **CRASH** (corticosteroids **increase mortality** in traumatic brain injury) and **DEX-CSDH** (dexamethasone gives **worse** outcomes in chronic subdural haematoma) — so dexamethasone is confined to vasogenic oedema around a tumour or abscess.

**⚠️ Process discovery worth keeping — the Markdown-lite renderer supports NEITHER ordered lists NOR indented sub-bullets.** `index.html`'s `MarkdownLite` handles only `##`/`###`, `-` and `*` bullets at column 0, `|` tables, `> ` quotes, `---`, `**bold**`, `*italic*` and backtick code. A line beginning `1. ` or `  - ` matches none of those and is swallowed into the **preceding paragraph**, so an eight-step escalation ladder renders as one run-on block. Two such spots were written and then caught before deployment (a numbered ladder in `raised_icp`, sub-bullets in `intracerebral_haemorrhage`) and converted to flat bullets; a file-wide scan confirms **no other topic in the notebook contains either construct**. Check for both when writing future batches.

**Cluster was put to the user, who took the recommendation.** *Peripheral nerve disorders* is already the app's home for cranial nerve lesions — Bell's palsy is a seventh nerve palsy and sits there — and, more precisely, Horner syndrome is there too and is the direct analogue: the other ptosis-plus-pupil syndrome with a localising differential. The alternatives offered were *Cerebrovascular disease* (filing it by its emergency, next to subarachnoid haemorrhage, since a painful pupil-involving palsy is a posterior communicating artery aneurysm until proved otherwise) and *Raised ICP & hydrocephalus* (uncal herniation being one cause).

**⚠️ Process note worth recording: the approved preview did not match the file, and the file was corrected to match the preview.** The preview shown to the user placed third nerve palsy **adjacent to Horner syndrome** — and that adjacency was half the argument for the cluster, since the two are the classic anisocoria pair (dilated versus constricted pupil, complete versus partial ptosis). The actual pre-existing array order was Bell's → carpal tunnel → Horner, so inserting after Bell's left carpal tunnel sitting between them. **`neuro_carpal` and `neuro_horner` were therefore swapped** by a reorder script that asserts the id multiset and the total byte length are both unchanged, so only position moved and no field text was touched. The cluster now reads **Bell's palsy → Third nerve palsy → Horner syndrome → Carpal tunnel syndrome** — the two cranial nerve palsies together, then the two pupil/ptosis syndromes together. **Lesson: build cluster previews from the actual array order, not from memory of it.**

**The entry is built around the pupil, and around the fact that the classic rule has named limits.** The parasympathetic fibres run on the OUTSIDE of the nerve, fed by pial vessels, while the core is fed by the vasa nervorum — so **compression hits the pupil fibres first (dilated pupil, "surgical") while microvascular ischaemia infarcts the core and spares them ("medical")**. The chart branches on that, then applies a safety check rather than treating it as absolute.

**⚠️ Verified on the web, and it refines what most textbooks say.** The "rule of the pupil" (around **97%** of aneurysmal third nerve palsies involve the pupil) can only be applied under **four conditions — Trobe's constraints**: the palsy must be **complete**, **isolated**, the pupil **fully spared rather than partially involved**, and the patient **over 50**. Fail any one and image regardless. An aneurysm can occasionally spare the pupil, so modern practice is closer to **imaging essentially everyone, with the pupil determining the URGENCY rather than whether to image at all** — the flowchart routes all four failure modes straight back to angiography. Also captured: **pain occurs in BOTH compressive and microvascular palsies and does not reliably separate them** (a common misconception), CT/MR angiography is **96% sensitive for aneurysms of 7 mm or more** (the size needed to compress the nerve), and **myasthenia gravis is the great mimic — fatigable, variable, and NEVER involves the pupil**.

Other content: the "down and out" eye from unopposed lateral rectus and superior oblique; **complete** ptosis versus Horner's partial; giant cell arteritis as an under-considered cause over 50; recovery expected within **3 months** for microvascular palsy, with failure to recover being the trigger to image; strabismus surgery only once stable beyond **6 months**; and **aberrant regeneration** (lid retraction on downgaze, pupil constriction on adduction) as itself a marker that the cause was compressive.

**Cluster was put to the user, and the answer expanded the job.** The options offered were *Headache & facial pain* (the recommendation — IIH already lives there and is literally raised ICP without a mass lesion, and "headache with papilloedema: mass or IIH?" is the high-yield decision), *CNS tumours & infection*, or a new cluster. **The user chose the new cluster AND asked for hydrocephalus to be added alongside**, which is the right call — hydrocephalus was in Batch 20's notebook scope but was not yet a Tracker condition at all, so the cluster starts with two members rather than one. **Third new cluster added to an already-clustered specialty** (after Neurology's *Spinal cord & autonomic disorders* and Gastro's *Neuroendocrine tumours*).

**Why the cluster is the honest answer:** raised ICP is a final common pathway rather than a disease of one system, and its causes were already scattered across four existing clusters — CNS tumours, subarachnoid haemorrhage, subdural haematoma and IIH. No existing home could hold it without misfiling it.

**Raised ICP** — 15 nodes, `source: "Neurosurgical emergency"`. Built on the Monro-Kellie doctrine and the perfusion equation (CPP = MAP − ICP, target 60–70 mmHg), then the escalation ladder. Points deliberately carried: the headache pattern (worse on waking, lying flat, coughing or straining); the **sixth nerve palsy as a FALSE localising sign**; that **Cushing's triad is LATE and pre-terminal**, not an early warning; the absolute rule that **a lumbar puncture must not be done where a mass lesion is suspected** (coning); and that head-up 30 degrees with a **neutral neck** matters mechanically.

**⚠️ Two management points verified on the web rather than recalled, both of which have moved:**
- **Hypertonic saline is now preferred over mannitol** for osmotherapy in traumatic brain injury and intracranial haemorrhage — a shift from the older mannitol-first teaching. Mannitol remains dosed at 0.25–1 g/kg, avoiding a systolic below 90.
- **Dexamethasone is for vasogenic oedema around a tumour or abscess ONLY, and must not be used in traumatic brain injury** — the CRASH trial showed corticosteroids **increase mortality** in head injury — nor in ischaemic stroke. This is a genuine safety point and is an explicit amber node on the chart.

Hyperventilation is framed as a **temporising bridge only** (PaCO2 4.0–4.5 kPa), since the vasoconstriction that lowers pressure also causes ischaemia and the effect wears off within hours.

**Hydrocephalus** — 17 nodes, `source: "Neurosurgical"`. The chart branches first on the three quite different presentations (infant / acute / insidious elderly), because they are almost separate diseases clinically, then on the split that decides safety: **communicating versus non-communicating**, because **lumbar puncture is absolutely contraindicated in obstructive hydrocephalus** but is the diagnostic test in normal pressure hydrocephalus. Content: infant signs (head circumference crossing centiles, bulging fontanelle, splayed sutures, **"sunsetting" eyes**); the NPH triad as **"wet, wacky and wobbly"** with **gait first and most reversible**; the **Evans index above 0.3, disproportionate to cortical atrophy** (large ventricles in a shrunken brain are atrophy, not hydrocephalus); the **tap test with objective gait assessment before and after**, which helps around **75%** of selected patients when shunted; **endoscopic third ventriculostomy** as the hardware-free alternative in obstructive disease, especially aqueduct stenosis; and the three shunt complications — infection, blockage, and **over-drainage causing low-pressure headache and subdural haematoma**.

**⚠️ Vitamin B12 deficiency was requested alongside it and was NOT added — it already exists twice.** `gi_b12_folate` "B12 & folate deficiency" sits in `gastro`'s *Small bowel & malabsorption* cluster, and `haem_b12_anaemia` "Pernicious anaemia" in `haematology`'s *Anaemias* cluster. The user confirmed the skip. This is the standing convention (melanoma, retinal detachment, ITP and hereditary angioedema were all skipped the same way) — **always duplicate-check before adding, including under alternative names**; "vitamin B12 deficiency" would not have been found by searching for its own title.

**Cluster was put to the user before writing.** Options offered were *Developmental & nutritional disorders* (the recommendation), *Spinal cord & autonomic disorders* (anatomically literal, but that cluster is four acute structural emergencies needing imaging within hours, which a chronic metabolic myelopathy sits oddly among), and *Demyelinating & neuromuscular disease* (defensible on mechanism, and SACD is a genuine MS mimic). **The user took the recommendation**, and it was inserted **directly after `neuro_wernicke`** — so the two vitamin-deficiency neurological syndromes now sit together, which is the point: both are "remember to check the level" diagnoses that are reversible if caught early.

**Built around the two things that make the diagnosis, both web-verified:**
- **The mixed upper and lower motor neurone picture** — brisk knee reflexes with **absent ankle reflexes and extensor plantars** — which looks contradictory until you realise the cord lesion and a peripheral neuropathy coexist from the same deficiency.
- **A normal serum B12 does not exclude it.** **RECREATIONAL NITROUS OXIDE is now a major cause in young adults** (median age around 24 in recent case series, typically daily use over weeks to months) and causes a **functional** deficiency by irreversibly oxidising the cobalt ion in cobalamin — so the measured B12 is often normal. **METHYLMALONIC ACID and HOMOCYSTEINE** are what reveal it. N2O-induced disease is **more severe and more extensive** than dietary or malabsorptive deficiency, and heavy use also carries a venous thromboembolism risk through hyperhomocysteinaemia.

Also carried: **never replace folate before B12**, which can precipitate or worsen the cord degeneration; the BNF regimen for neurological involvement, which is more intensive than the standard one — **hydroxocobalamin 1 mg IM on ALTERNATE DAYS until no further improvement, then 1 mg every 2 months** (versus three times weekly for two weeks then 3-monthly without neurological signs); the MRI **"inverted V" / "inverted rabbit ears"** dorsal-column sign, with the instruction not to let imaging delay treatment; and **copper deficiency** as the near-identical myelopathy mimic.

**Roadmap consequence:** SACD is a new Tracker condition in a cluster the neurology batches did not previously account for, so **Batch 23 now also owns it** — it is a myelopathy and belongs in the notebook alongside the other cord syndromes even though the Tracker files it under nutritional. The roadmap row has been updated.

**Source is `"Society for Endocrinology"`**, matching its `endo_hypocalcaemia` sibling. Written against the actual SfE Endocrine Emergency Guidance PDF (Walsh, Gittoes and Selby, *Endocrine Connections* 2016), downloaded and read rather than recalled, so the thresholds and doses are the guideline's own.

**⚠️ One piece of classic teaching that the guidance explicitly contradicts, and the reason this entry was worth doing carefully: FUROSEMIDE IS NO LONGER PART OF THE PATHWAY.** Loop diuretics were historically given routinely to promote calciuresis. The SfE guidance states they are **rarely used, only if fluid overload develops, and are NOT effective at reducing serum calcium** — the citation behind that is a narrative review titled "furosemide for hypercalcemia: an unproven yet common practice". Giving them to a volume-deplete patient makes matters worse. The flowchart carries this as an explicit amber node rather than leaving it implicit.

**Structure — severity triage, then rehydration, then the PTH split.** The guideline's own three bands drive the chart: **below 3.0 mmol/L** often asymptomatic and not needing urgent correction, **3.0–3.5** often symptomatic with prompt treatment usually indicated, **above 3.5** urgent because of dysrhythmia and coma. Then **IV 0.9% sodium chloride 4–6 L over 24 hours** as the single most important intervention — and the entry explains *why*: hypercalcaemia causes a nephrogenic diabetes insipidus, and the resulting volume depletion itself reduces renal calcium excretion, so rehydration breaks a vicious cycle. Then **zoledronic acid 4 mg over 15 minutes** if still hypercalcaemic, with the point that the **nadir is at 2–4 days** so failure must not be judged too early, and dose reduction plus slower infusion in renal impairment.

**Scoped against the existing `endo_hyperparathyroid` entry using the umbrella/subset rule** (the same pattern as `endo_hyperthyroid` versus `endo_graves`): hypercalcaemia owns the **acute emergency and the cause-finding split**, and hyperparathyroidism keeps the parathyroid disease itself. The pivot is **PTH — raised or inappropriately normal means PTH-driven (primary or tertiary hyperparathyroidism), suppressed means PTH-independent and therefore malignancy until proved otherwise**, with the three malignant mechanisms named (PTH-related peptide from squamous tumours, osteolytic metastases, and 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D from lymphoma). The chart hands off to the Hyperparathyroidism entry rather than repeating it.

**Other high-yield content:** **familial hypocalciuric hypercalcaemia** as the mimic that matters — hypercalcaemia with an inappropriately normal or raised PTH, easily mislabelled as primary hyperparathyroidism, benign, and **must not be operated on**, distinguished by a urinary calcium:creatinine clearance ratio **below 0.01**; the **albumin adjustment** and why it matters in exactly this population (a low albumin in malignancy, sepsis or liver disease masks a high ionised calcium); the **shortened QT** as the ECG mirror-image of hypocalcaemia; **prednisolone 40 mg daily** for the 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D-mediated causes, effective within 2–4 days; and **rebound hypocalcaemia after a bisphosphonate** where vitamin D is deficient or PTH suppressed.

**Written to match the Tracker's `gi_masld`, so the two now agree.** Everything was web-verified before writing rather than recalled, and two things came back differently from what the brief assumed:
- **Semaglutide (Wegovy) was authorised on 3 July 2026, not June, and as a CONDITIONAL marketing authorisation** — resmetirom (Rezdiffra) is the **3 June 2026** one. Both dates and the conditional status are from the MHRA's own announcements. The topic gives each drug its own date. The GOV.UK semaglutide page states outright that patients cannot get it on the NHS for this indication and that a NICE appraisal is underway; the British Liver Trust confirms the same for both drugs.
- **EASL 2024 does NOT recommend routine HCC surveillance at F3** — surveillance belongs to cirrhosis (F4), and at F3 it is decided case by case on individual risk. The topic says this rather than implying blanket F3 surveillance. It still states that MASLD-related HCC can arise without cirrhosis, which the guideline supports (cirrhosis absent in 38–49% of MASLD-HCC in the cited series).

**Also picked up, and worth knowing: NG49 is being replaced.** NICE has an update in progress (GID-NG10434) with the replacement guideline **expected in July 2027**, and it will adopt **MASLD and MASH** in the title and throughout, because NAFLD and NASH are now considered inaccurate and stigmatising. That is why the ELF test and the old name are still what UK practice and exams use today — the topic says so plainly, without any "the guide was wrong" framing.

**Numbers added, with sources:** FIB-4 rule-out **below 1.3** (below 2.0 if over 65) and transient elastography **under 8 kPa** rules out / **8–12 kPa** indeterminate / **12 kPa or above** rules in (EASL-EASD-EASO 2024); ELF **10.51 or above**, retest at **3 years** in adults and **2 years** in children (NICE NG49); the weight-loss dose-response **~5% steatosis / 7–10% MASH resolution / 10%+ fibrosis regression**, with fibrosis regressing in about **45%** of those reaching 10% (Vilar-Gomez, *Gastroenterology* 2015); **resmetirom 80 mg daily below 100 kg, 100 mg at or above**, MASH resolution **26–30% vs 10%** (MAESTRO-NASH); **semaglutide ESSENCE at 72 weeks, MASH resolution 62.9% vs 34.1% and fibrosis improvement 37% vs 22.5%**; **vitamin E 800 IU daily** in selected non-diabetic adults with biopsy-proven MASH, with the prostate-cancer and haemorrhagic-stroke cautions; pioglitazone's weight gain, fluid retention, heart-failure and fracture cautions.

**Statins get their own section and a blunt line — safe in MASLD, must not be withheld** — because the common error is stopping them for mildly raised transaminases, and **cardiovascular disease, not liver disease, is the leading cause of death**. Only decompensated cirrhosis warrants caution.

Options offered: *Tubulointerstitial & vascular* (the recommendation), a new *Tubular & acid-base disorders* cluster, or *Infection, stones & haematuria* (on the type 1 nephrocalcinosis/stones link). **The user took the recommendation**, and it was inserted **directly after `renal_interstitial_nephritis`** — a deliberate pairing, because interstitial nephritis is itself a recognised cause of type 1 RTA.

**Fields are split by type, at the user's request — a new pattern for the Tracker.** All six of `aetiology`, `pathophysiology`, `presentation`, `investigations`, `treatment` and `complications` now render as four lines each: a shared opening line, then **Type 1 (distal)**, **Type 2 (proximal)** and **Type 4 (hyperkalaemic)**. This works because every Tracker field row renders with `whiteSpace: "pre-line"`, so a literal `\n` becomes a line break — the same mechanism `cvs_mi` uses for its timed complications list, applied here to the whole entry. **Type 3 is deliberately held to two clauses** (one in `epidemiology`, one closing line in `aetiology` noting it is carbonic anhydrase II deficiency and not exam-relevant), per the user's instruction to keep it scant given its rarity. The insertion script asserts that all three relevant types appear in each of the six split fields.

**The flowchart is built as a diagnostic ladder rather than a treatment pathway**, because that is what the subject actually tests — three sequential discriminators, each an explicit decision node: **urinary anion gap** (negative = gastrointestinal bicarbonate loss such as diarrhoea, which is far commoner than RTA overall; positive = renal) → **serum potassium** (HIGH = type 4, the only hyperkalaemic type and the commonest in adults) → **urine pH during acidosis** (above 5.5 = type 1 distal, cannot acidify; below 5.5 = type 2 proximal, wastes bicarbonate until the serum level falls). Each type then gets its own workup and treatment branch.

**Two points verified on the web that refine the classic teaching:** **fludrocortisone in type 4 is second-line, not the default** — it corrects the underlying deficiency and works, but worsens hypertension, oedema and heart failure in exactly the diabetic and CKD patients who get type 4, so management leads on stopping the culprit drug, dietary potassium restriction and a loop diuretic. And **type 1 alkali should be given as a POTASSIUM salt (potassium citrate, ~1 mmol/kg/day)**, correcting the acidosis and the hypokalaemia together. Source label **"AJKD Core Curriculum"** — there is no NICE or UK guideline for RTA, and the AJKD Core Curriculum (2025) is the current authoritative synthesis.

**Source is deliberately dual — `"NICE NG49 / EASL-EASD-EASO"` — because the UK and international pathways have diverged, and the flowchart shows both.** Verified on the web:
- **NICE NG49 is still current, still called "Non-alcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD)", and still uses the ELF test** — a score of **10.51 or above** indicates advanced fibrosis and warrants referral, with retesting every **3 years** in adults (2 in children) below that.
- **EASL-EASD-EASO 2024** uses the new naming and a stepwise **FIB-4 first, then transient elastography** approach, which is what has largely become practice because FIB-4 is free and calculable from routine bloods.

The chart therefore branches explicitly on which pathway is being followed rather than pretending they agree.

**⚠️ Genuinely new UK development, and the thing I would have got wrong from memory — two MASH drugs were licensed in the UK this year.** The **MHRA authorised resmetirom (Rezdiffra) on 3 June 2026** for MASH with **stage F2–F3 fibrosis** — the first medicine ever licensed in the UK specifically for MASH — and also authorised **semaglutide** for MASH with moderate to advanced fibrosis, on the strength of the **ESSENCE** phase 3 trial. **Both are awaiting NICE technology appraisal, so neither is routinely available on the NHS yet**, and the entry says so rather than implying they are standard care.

**Other content worth flagging:** the entry leads on the change that came with the 2023 renaming — **MASLD requires POSITIVE metabolic criteria (at least one of five cardiometabolic factors), where the old NAFLD was a diagnosis of exclusion**; that **fibrosis stage, not steatosis, determines outcome**; that **transaminases are often normal and a normal ALT does not exclude advanced fibrosis**; that **statins are safe and must not be withheld**, because **cardiovascular disease — not liver disease — is the leading cause of death in MASLD**; and that **MASLD-related HCC can arise without cirrhosis**, unusually among the chronic liver diseases. Weight-loss targets are given as the dose-response that gets examined: ~5% improves steatosis, 7–10% achieves MASH resolution, 10%+ can regress fibrosis.

Emergency first, then the milder cousin. Flowcharts 13 nodes each, **`source: "WSES / ESVS"`** and **`"ACG"`**.

**The two ischaemic entries are deliberately built as a contrasting pair, because that contrast is what gets examined.** Acute mesenteric ischaemia is small bowel, superior mesenteric artery, catastrophic, 50–80% mortality; ischaemic colitis is colon, watershed, usually self-limiting with recovery in 1–2 weeks. Each chart cross-references the other at the point where they can be confused.

**Content worth flagging:**
- **AMI is built around the sign, not the test**: WSES states that severe pain **out of proportion to the examination** should be *assumed* to be AMI until proved otherwise. The chart makes the point that **a normal lactate does NOT exclude it** — lactate rises only once bowel is infarcting, and using it as a screen is how the diagnosis gets missed. **CT angiography without delay.** The management then splits three ways on mechanism (arterial occlusion / venous thrombosis / non-occlusive low flow), which is the whole reason the CT angiogram matters.
- **A genuine shift in AMI practice was verified and written in**: for arterial occlusion **without** peritonitis, **endovascular revascularisation is now the primary option** where expertise exists, rather than open embolectomy. Overt peritonitis still means prompt laparotomy. ESVS supports **short-term dual antiplatelet** after mesenteric revascularisation. Note **ESVS published new mesenteric/renal artery guidelines in 2025**, alongside the WSES 2022 update — both were checked.
- **Ischaemic colitis carries the safety check that matters**: **isolated RIGHT-sided colonic ischaemia, or severe pain WITHOUT bleeding**, is superior-mesenteric territory, carries a far worse prognosis, and should be treated as possible AMI. Also the anatomy — **splenic flexure (Griffiths' point) and rectosigmoid junction (Sudeck's point)**, with the **rectum spared** by its dual internal iliac supply, so rectal involvement should make you doubt the diagnosis — and that **pneumatosis or porto-mesenteric venous gas predicts transmural infarction**.
- **Kallmann is structured around the discriminator, not the syndrome**: the chart starts at delayed puberty and splits on the **gonadotrophin pattern** — HIGH LH/FSH means primary gonadal failure (karyotype for Klinefelter or Turner), LOW or inappropriately normal means the hypothalamic-pituitary axis, which is where Kallmann sits. Smell is then the pivot (anosmia in about half of congenital hypogonadotropic hypogonadism). It also carries the two things most often missed: **constitutional delay is the commonest cause of delayed puberty** and must be separated first, and **reversal occurs in 10–15%**, so treatment should be interrupted periodically to retest rather than assumed lifelong. Fertility needs **gonadotrophins (hCG then FSH) or pulsatile GnRH** — sex steroids alone will never restore it.

Management verified on the web rather than recalled: WSES 2022 and ESVS 2025 for mesenteric ischaemia; the ACG colon ischaemia guideline (early colonoscopy within 48 hours, conservative mainstay, pneumatosis/portal venous gas as infarction predictors); and the European Consensus Statement on congenital hypogonadotropic hypogonadism plus the Endocrine Reviews clinical management paper.

Built around the two things that get examined: it is a **diagnosis of exclusion** (check a glucose in every confused cirrhotic, and exclude sepsis, intracranial bleed, alcohol withdrawal and Wernicke's — the last two are common in exactly this population and treated completely differently), and **finding the precipitant IS the treatment** (infection with an ascitic tap for SBP, GI bleed, constipation, dehydration, hypokalaemia, sedatives, renal impairment, TIPSS).

**Cluster choice was put to the user before writing, with three options: *Small bowel & malabsorption* (the recommendation — the classic midgut/terminal-ileal primary, and it would have balanced the thinnest cluster), *Lower GI & colorectal* (the appendix is the commonest site overall), or a new *Neuroendocrine tumours* cluster. The user chose the new cluster.** This is the **second time a brand-new cluster has been added to an already-clustered specialty** — the first was Neurology's *Spinal cord & autonomic disorders*. It has room to grow: pancreatic neuroendocrine tumours (gastrinoma, insulinoma, VIPoma) and MEN would sit here naturally if ever added.

**Why two separate carcinoid entries rather than one, and how the scope was split** (the umbrella/subset rule): the **tumour** entry owns the oncological pathway — chromogranin A and urinary 5-HIAA, **68Ga-DOTATATE PET-CT (which has replaced octreotide scintigraphy)**, Ki-67 grading into G1/G2/G3, resection as the only cure with the heavily-examined **appendiceal size rule (under 1 cm appendicectomy alone, over 2 cm right hemicolectomy)**, then somatostatin analogues for antiproliferative control and **PRRT with lutetium-177 DOTATATE** for progressive receptor-positive disease. The **syndrome** entry owns the endocrine pathway and hinges on the concept that makes it make sense: **mediators from a gut tumour are inactivated by the liver, so the syndrome implies liver metastases or a primary draining systemically (bronchial, ovarian)**. It carries carcinoid heart disease (**right-sided — tricuspid regurgitation and pulmonary stenosis, the left heart spared because the lungs inactivate the mediators**), **telotristat ethyl** for somatostatin-refractory diarrhoea, **niacin for pellagra** from tryptophan diversion, and **carcinoid crisis — IV octreotide and AVOID catecholamines**, with prophylactic octreotide before any procedure.

Management verified on the web rather than recalled: **ENETS 2024 guidance for well-differentiated small intestine NETs** and ENETS 2023 for colorectal; 68Ga-DOTATATE PET-CT as the current somatostatin receptor imaging standard; PRRT as standard second-line with **NETTER-2 (2024)** supporting earlier use in higher-grade disease; and for hepatic encephalopathy that **protein restriction is now explicitly advised against** (the classic outdated teaching) with a target of **1.2 to 1.5 g/kg/day**, lactulose titrated to stool frequency, and rifaximin per **NICE TA337** after a second overt episode.
