Earlier: **Rebuilt the `endo_dka` Diabetic ketoacidosis flowchart (11→16 nodes) and re-sourced it to JBDS-IP (March 2023).** Prompted by the user spotting that the chart forked after fluid resuscitation into two unlabelled branches with nothing explaining why. Verified against the actual guideline PDF (`abcd.care`, JBDS 02, revised March 2023 — confirmed on the web as the current version; the 2024 international hyperglycaemic-crises consensus report incorporates it but does not supersede it).

**The unlabelled fork — what it was and what it is now.** The old chart ran `C --> D` (fluids → insulin) *and* `C --> K` (fluids → treat the precipitant, VTE prophylaxis, senior review) as two bare arrows off the same node, so it read as a decision the reader could not resolve. It was never a decision — the second arm is concurrent supportive care. **Every fork in the rebuilt chart is now labelled**, and the parallel arm leaves the pathway at the top (`B -- "in parallel" --> O`) with its node label starting **"IN PARALLEL from the outset"**, against `B -- "main pathway" --> C` for the resuscitation sequence. A static check in the rebuild script now asserts that no node with more than one outgoing edge has an unlabelled arrow — it caught one remaining case during the rebuild.

**The chart was also missing the split the guideline actually makes at that point.** JBDS ACTION 5 branches on **shocked versus not shocked**, and the old chart had it only buried in a tooltip. It is now an explicit decision node: **systolic BP under 90 → 500 mL 0.9% sodium chloride over 15 minutes, repeated if systolic is still under 100**, versus **not shocked → 1 L over 1 hour** then the JBDS schedule.

**Other gaps closed, all verified against the guideline PDF rather than recalled:**
- **The escalation rule was absent.** If the hourly targets are not met (ketones falling 0.5 mmol/L/h, bicarbonate rising 3 mmol/L/h, glucose falling 3 mmol/L/h), **check the pump and lines FIRST** — a disconnected or malfunctioning pump is the commonest cause — then increase the fixed-rate infusion in **1 unit/h** increments. This is now a decision node with a feedback loop.
- **A real logic bug: the old "not resolved" arrow looped back to the node that starts the infusion at 0.1 units/kg/h**, which would have undone the reduction to 0.05. It now loops back to the monitoring node.
- **10% glucose rate added — 125 mL/h** (the guideline body's figure; note the example monitoring chart in the appendix says 100 mL/h, so the body text was taken as authoritative).
- **Severe-DKA criteria for critical care** promoted from a tooltip into a node: ketones above 6, pH below 7.0, bicarbonate below 5, potassium below 3.5, GCS below 12.
- **Potassium thresholds** now on the chart (40 mmol/L if 3.5–5.5, none above 5.5, senior advice below 3.5), with "**do not give if anuric**" and the 20 mmol/h cardiac-monitoring rule in the tooltip.
- The **"do not use bicarbonate as the resolution marker"** caveat added — large-volume 0.9% sodium chloride causes a hyperchloraemic acidosis that keeps bicarbonate low after the ketosis has cleared.

**Resolution threshold checked and left unchanged at ketones <0.6 AND venous pH >7.3.** Worth recording because the guideline is internally inconsistent: the **example local monitoring chart in the appendix says "ketones <0.3 and/or pH >7.3"**, but the guideline body states **<0.6 AND pH >7.3** consistently across Sections B, C, D and the conversion-to-subcutaneous section. The body text was taken as authoritative.

Earlier: **Brought `immunity_vaccination` (`immunology`) current with the 1 January 2026 childhood immunisation schedule — this CLEARS the stale-guidance flag raised in Batch 16e, and no stale-guidance flags now remain open.** Re-verified on the web before writing rather than recalled, against the GOV.UK MMRV programme letter, the complete routine immunisation schedule from 1 January 2026, the UKHSA schedule-change briefing and the MMRV healthcare-professional guidance. **No topic id changed and none added or removed (total stays 212)**, so all bookmarks survive; this is an in-place rewrite of two sections plus one table cell.

**The change turned out to be wider than the MMR→MMRV swap the flag described — three things moved at once on 1 January 2026, and the old text was wrong about all three:**

- **MMRV replaced MMR** in the routine programme, putting varicella into the schedule for the first time. The topic now carries the full **birth-cohort table**, because which doses a child is offered depends on when they were born: born **1 Jan 2025 onwards** → MMRV at 12 and 18 months; born **1 Jul–31 Dec 2024** → MMR at 12 months, then MMRV at 18 months *and* again at 3 years 4 months (they need two varicella-containing doses, having had plain MMR first); born **1 Sep 2022–30 Jun 2024** → a single MMRV at 3 years 4 months; born **1 Jan 2020–31 Aug 2022** → selective catch-up only, running **1 November 2026 to 31 March 2028**, if there is no chickenpox history and not already two varicella doses.
- **The Haemophilus influenzae type b/meningococcal C booster was withdrawn from the one-year visit** — the old text still listed it. Menitorix, its only brand, is no longer manufactured; teenage MenACWY gives herd protection against group C; and the Hib component moved into a fourth 6-in-1 dose.
- **There is a new 18-month appointment** (children born on or after 1 July 2024): the fourth 6-in-1 dose plus the second MMRV dose, brought forward from 3 years 4 months. The pre-school visit keeps the 4-in-1 booster.

**Two further staleness fixes in the same block, since it was being rewritten anyway:** the infant bullet now gives the **MenB 8-and-12-week / PCV 16-week** timings (MenB's second dose moved forward from 16 weeks, PCV's first moved back from 12 weeks), and the adult bullet's **"shingles from 70" was stale** — shingles and pneumococcal are both **from 65** now, with **RSV at 75**, and pregnancy carries **RSV from 28 weeks** alongside flu at any stage and pertussis from 16 weeks.

**Added beyond what the flag asked for:** the two products (**ProQuad and Priorix-Tetra** — clinically equivalent and interchangeable, with Priorix-Tetra offered where porcine gelatine is declined, because ProQuad contains it), and the **febrile convulsion** point that is the obvious exam hook — roughly **1 in 1,000 after the FIRST dose** at 7 to 10 days, about **double** separate MMR plus varicella given at the same visit, but far below the **1 in 43** that follows natural measles, with **no excess after the second dose** and **prophylactic paracetamol not recommended**. Per the standing convention none of this is framed as an update inside the topic — the corrected facts are simply stated plainly, and the record of what changed lives only here.

**The Key safety rule section was reworked** as requested: it now gives an explicit live-vaccine list with **varicella and MMRV in it**, notes that the live-vaccine rule therefore now bites at the **12-month and 18-month appointments** rather than only later in life, sends immunosuppressed children needing varicella cover to specialist advice rather than MMRV, and refines the HIV line to **MMR and MMRV where immunosuppression is absent or moderate** (a CD4 count above 500 in a child aged 1 to 5, after specialist discussion) but still never BCG or yellow fever. The live-attenuated row of the **Types of vaccine** table now reads "MMR and MMRV".

No id changed and nothing existing moved.

- **`gi_achalasia` Achalasia** → Gastro's *Oesophagus & upper GI* cluster, inserted **after `gi_oesophageal_ca`**. That keeps the reflux → Barrett's → adenocarcinoma chain intact at the head of the cluster and puts achalasia next to the other benign dysphagia entry (`gi_pharyngeal_pouch`), with cancer immediately before it — which is exactly the clinical relationship, because **pseudoachalasia (tumour at the gastro-oesophageal junction) is the thing endoscopy must exclude first**. Flowchart 15 nodes, **`source: "ASGE / SAGES"`** — verified on the web that **there is no UK national guideline for achalasia**, so the pathway follows the ASGE 2020 guideline and the SAGES 2024 POEM update. Pathway: endoscopy first to exclude malignancy → high-resolution manometry as the gold standard → Chicago subtype → **type III goes to POEM** (the myotomy can be extended along the spastic segment, which dilatation and a standard Heller myotomy cannot do) versus type I/II choosing between pneumatic dilatation, Heller myotomy with partial fundoplication and POEM → botulinum toxin or nitrates/calcium channel blockers only if unfit → Eckardt score of 3 or less as the definition of success. **This closes the last of the three oesophageal gaps flagged when `gi_oesophageal_ca` was written** (varices and Barrett's were done in Batch 16-era work).
- **`id_sbp` Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis** → Infectious Diseases' *Gastrointestinal infection* cluster, placed **last, after `id_giardiasis`**: the rest of that cluster is luminal/enteric infection, so SBP sits at the end as the intra-abdominal entity rather than being interleaved. Flowchart 15 nodes, **`source: "EASL / NICE NG50"`**.

**⚠️ Guideline correction — NICE NG50's SBP prophylaxis recommendation was REVERSED and the old version is what most sources still teach.** Verified on the web, not recalled. NG50 used to say to offer prophylactic ciprofloxacin or norfloxacin to anyone with cirrhosis and ascitic protein of 15 g/L or less until the ascites resolved. **The updated NG50 says: do NOT routinely offer antibiotics to prevent SBP** — reserve prophylaxis for those at high risk from severe liver disease (for example ascitic protein 15 g/L or less, **Child-Pugh above 9, or MELD above 16**) or where an infection would seriously affect their care. Two drivers: **norfloxacin has been withdrawn in the UK**, and the MHRA fluoroquinolone safety warnings. The flowchart states the current position on the primary-prophylaxis branch; **ciprofloxacin is the UK agent** for secondary prophylaxis. Also verified: the albumin regimen is **1.5 g/kg on day 1 and 1 g/kg on day 3**, which is not an optional extra — adding it to cefotaxime cut hepatorenal syndrome from 30% to 10% and mortality from 29% to 10%, with the clearest benefit at creatinine 88 micromol/L or more or bilirubin 68 micromol/L or more.

Both entries deliberately carry the **subtle-presentation** and **diagnostic-trap** points that get examined: for SBP, that **up to a third have neither fever nor abdominal pain** and it may show only as new encephalopathy or worsening renal function, that the **neutrophil count of 250 cells per cubic mm is the diagnosis** (culture is negative in about 40% — culture-negative neutrocytic ascites, treated identically), and the **secondary-peritonitis** discriminators; for achalasia, **dysphagia to solids AND liquids from the outset** (versus solids-first in mechanical obstruction) and the pseudoachalasia red flags.

**⚠️ This was by far the most guideline-drifted block so far — FIVE separate corrections, and one whole topic in the guide is superseded.** All fixed silently in the notebook per the standing convention; recorded here:

1. **Bacterial meningitis: the guide follows NICE CKS 2020, which is superseded by NICE NG240 (March 2024, replacing CG102).** Four substantive changes, all verified on the NICE guideline itself: (a) the guide's **"under 3 months: ampicillin/amoxicillin + cefotaxime; over 3 months: ceftriaxone"** split is gone — **ceftriaxone is now first-line at every age**, with **amoxicillin added for Listeria risk** and **cefotaxime only where ceftriaxone is contraindicated** (notably pre-term babies under 41 weeks corrected gestational age); (b) the guide's **"penicillin allergy → moxifloxacin and vancomycin" is wrong** — NG240 specifies **chloramphenicol** for severe beta-lactam allergy (plus co-trimoxazole if Listeria risk), and says a *non-severe* allergy is not a reason to avoid ceftriaxone; (c) **the guide's four CSF criteria for dexamethasone (frankly purulent, WCC >1000/µL, WCC + protein >1 g/L, bacteria on Gram stain) are CG102 and have been dropped** — NG240 gives dexamethasone to **everyone over 3 months with strongly suspected or confirmed bacterial meningitis**, continued only for pneumococcus or Hib and stopped for other organisms; (d) the guide's **"recent foreign travel: add vancomycin" was a CG102 recommendation and is not in NG240** — deliberately omitted. The guide was *right* about no steroids in meningococcal disease, ciprofloxacin over rifampicin for contacts, and the 4-to-6-week paediatrician review; NG240's **audiology within 4 weeks (preferably before discharge)** and the 1-year and 2-year follow-up were added.
2. **Tepid sponging — the guide recommends it TWICE and NICE NG143 explicitly does not.** The guide says external cooling "can use warm sponging to reduce temperature rapidly" under Fever, and "paracetamol and tepid sponging can be used" under Dengue. **NG143: "Tepid sponging is not recommended."** The full NG143 antipyretic rules were written in instead (distress not temperature, never both agents simultaneously, switch rather than combine, alternate only if distress recurs, and antipyretics do not prevent febrile convulsions).
3. **The UK childhood vaccination schedule changed underneath this block.** Since **1 January 2026 the routine schedule uses the combined MMRV vaccine**, adding varicella: **two doses at 12 and 18 months**, with the second dose **brought forward from 3 years 4 months**, plus a catch-up programme running **November 2026 to March 2028**. The guide predates this entirely. Written into both `measles_mumps_rubella` and `paediatric_chickenpox`. **✅ RESOLVED 2026-07-29 — the `immunity_vaccination` topic in `immunology` was made stale by this and has now been rewritten (see the top entry). No stale-guidance flags remain open.**
4. **Typhoid: the guide's "known → ciprofloxacin 7 days" is unsafe for South Asian acquisition**, where fluoroquinolone resistance is now widespread. Written as **azithromycin or ceftriaxone** as the reliable choices with ciprofloxacin reserved for a known-sensitive isolate, plus the **extensively drug-resistant (XDR)** strains from Pakistan needing azithromycin and/or a carbapenem.
5. **Malaria: the guide omits primaquine entirely.** Treating *P. vivax* and *P. ovale* without **primaquine radical cure of the liver hypnozoites** guarantees relapse, and **G6PD must be checked before giving it**. Added. Also corrected the guide's **"cyclospirin" → ciclosporin** (Kawasaki) and its US-only **oxacillin/nafcillin** in toxic shock syndrome → **flucloxacillin** for UK practice, keeping the US names as a parenthetical.

**How the four existing owners were handled** (the standing rule: check for an owner, carry only the paediatric-specific material, cross-reference). `paediatric_encephalitis` cross-references the thorough adult `encephalitis` in `neurology` for the pathology, HSV temporal-lobe findings and anti-NMDA disease, carrying only the paediatric management — the antiviral-by-virus table, ICU care and the 1-year follow-up. `paediatric_tuberculosis` cross-references `infectious/tuberculosis` for microbiology, latency and drug toxicities, carrying **gastric lavage rather than sputum, paucibacillary smear-negative disease, the under-5 risk of miliary/CNS spread, TB meningitis at 12 months with dexamethasone**, and the WHO 4-month SHINE-trial regimen for non-severe paediatric disease. `paediatric_hiv` cross-references the four adult HIV topics and `pregnancy_uti_gbs`. `paediatric_immunodeficiency` cross-references `immunology/immunodeficiency` for the classification and investigations, carrying only prophylaxis, treatment and transplant/gene-therapy management.

**1. `fatty_liver_disease` (Batch 13) updated to the 2023 steatotic liver disease nomenclature.** Verified on the web rather than recalled. The multi-society Delphi consensus (**AASLD, EASL and ALEH, 2023**) did more than rename things, and the topic now reflects all of it:
- **NAFLD → MASLD** (metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease); **NASH → MASH**; **steatotic liver disease (SLD)** is the new umbrella term. An old-to-new mapping table makes the change explicit, since both names will be in circulation for years.
- **The substantive change, not just a relabelling:** MASLD requires hepatic steatosis **plus at least one of five cardiometabolic criteria** (adiposity, dysglycaemia, hypertension, raised triglycerides, low HDL). The old NAFLD was a diagnosis of *exclusion*; MASLD demands **positive evidence of metabolic dysfunction**.
- **A whole new category was added — `MetALD`**, for people meeting MASLD criteria who also drink more than a modest amount: **140–350 g/week in women, 210–420 g/week in men**, with alcohol-related liver disease above that. This is genuinely valuable here because the topic covers *both* alcohol-related and metabolic fatty liver, and **MetALD sits exactly between its two halves** — the old binary framing had nowhere to put a patient with both drivers.
- The **topic name** was updated from "Alcohol-related & metabolic (MASLD) fatty liver disease" to **"Alcohol-related & metabolic (MASLD) fatty liver disease"** (names are display-only, so the id and bookmarks are untouched), and the stale bare **"NAFLD"** in the hepatocellular carcinoma risk-factor list in `liver_tumours` was corrected too. A sweep confirmed **every remaining NAFLD/NASH mention in the notebook is now a deliberate "formerly" framing**, not a primary usage.

**2. `asthma_copd_compare` (Respiratory) updated to BTS/NICE/SIGN NG245.** It was only 1025 characters and predated the November 2024 rewrite — it mentioned MART but lacked everything else. Now 3548 characters and consistent with the paediatric topic written in Batch 16c(i):
- The **overriding safety rule**: never prescribe a SABA at any age without a concomitant ICS.
- **AIR** and **MART** defined, and the **12-and-over ladder** — as-needed AIR → low-dose MART → moderate-dose MART → check FeNO and eosinophils (refer if either raised) → time-limited **LTRA or LAMA** trial → refer.
- **Objective diagnosis added**, which the topic previously lacked entirely: eosinophils or **FeNO 50 ppb or more** first in adults, then bronchodilator reversibility, then peak flow variability, then bronchial challenge.
- The **LTRA demotion** to a time-limited trial with a stop rule, the **MHRA montelukast neuropsychiatric warning**, **LAMA as a new option**, and that **digital inhalers are not recommended for routine use**.
- Cross-references *Asthma in children* for the under-12 ladders. **The COPD half was left unchanged**, being consistent with NG115.

**Three things worth flagging as content:**
- **`gastroenteritis_and_fluid_management` carries all three fluid calculations**, which are heavily examined and appear nowhere else in the app: resuscitation at **20 mL/kg** (10–20 in term neonates), deficit as **% x weight x 10 over 48 hours**, and maintenance on the **100/50/20** rule — with the guide's worked example (a 20 kg child with 5% dehydration = 2000 mL/24 h, about 83 mL/hour), the neonatal day-1-to-28 volumes, and the rule that hypernatraemic dehydration must be corrected **slowly over at least 48 hours**.
- **Paediatric Crohn's differs sharply from adult practice** and this is the highest-yield contrast in the batch: **exclusive enteral nutrition for 12 weeks is first-line induction in low-risk children**, not corticosteroids, because it treats the growth failure as well; high-risk children (Paris classification) go **straight to anti-TNF — "top-down" therapy**, a shift driven by the **TISKIDS and REACH** trials.
- **Biliary atresia's 60-day window** and the rule that **any baby jaundiced beyond 14 days needs a split bilirubin** — the topic is deliberately placed to back up the pointer already made from `neonatal_jaundice`, which previously had nowhere to send the reader.

The new paediatric topic uses MASLD. **The adult `fatty_liver_disease` topic in `gastro` still uses NAFLD/NASH throughout** — it was written in Batch 13 before this came up. It is not wrong, but it is behind the current nomenclature; **offer to update it.**

**Cow's milk protein allergy and lactose intolerance were deliberately merged into one topic**, because the examinable point is telling them apart — an **immune** reaction to the protein versus an **enzymatic** lactase deficiency — and the discriminator is that only the allergy has extra-intestinal features. The topic opens on that contrast table before covering each management in full (IgE versus non-IgE onset windows, extensively hydrolysed then amino acid formula, maternal exclusion with calcium and vitamin D, the milk ladder, and the 2–3 weeks it takes to clear cow's milk protein from breast milk).

**⚠️ Guideline nuance found and captured — a deliberate national deviation from NICE.** The guide gives the standard NICE NG84 thresholds for treating sore throat (**FeverPAIN 4–5 or Centor 3–4**). Checking this on the web surfaced that **interim guidance from NHS England and UKHSA — endorsed by NICE, the RCGP, the RCPCH and the RPS — advises treating children at a FeverPAIN of 3 or more**, an explicitly *lower* threshold adopted after the rise in invasive group A streptococcal infection, and one the guidance itself notes "deviates from NICE guidance". Both thresholds are now written, clearly labelled. Also confirmed: penicillin V remains first choice with **amoxicillin, then a macrolide, then cefalexin** as the fallback order, and scarlet fever gets **10 days** (a 5-day course being acceptable at clinician discretion) because the duration is about preventing rheumatic fever and glomerulonephritis rather than shortening the illness.

**Guide error corrected silently:** p109 gives "**Cefalozin** AND cefalexin" for laryngitis with airway compromise — the drug is **cefazolin**.

Earlier: **Added Hashimoto's thyroiditis, Toxic multinodular goitre and Thyroid storm to the Endocrinology Tracker**, all three in the *Thyroid* cluster. None existed (`endo_thyrotoxicosis` is a broader, separate entity), so no duplicates. **This closes the "no thyroid storm" sibling gap flagged when Myxoedema coma was added** — the cluster now has both decompensation emergencies.

**Placement — each umbrella is now followed by its causes, then its emergency.** The three were inserted after `endo_hypothyroid`, `endo_graves` and `endo_thyrotoxicosis` respectively, so **no existing condition moved relative to another** and the cluster reads: Hypothyroidism → **Hashimoto's thyroiditis** → Myxoedema coma → Hyperthyroidism → Graves' disease → **Toxic multinodular goitre** → De Quervain's thyroiditis → Thyrotoxicosis → **Thyroid storm** → Thyroid eye disease. The three hyperthyroid causes now sit in **radionuclide uptake order** (diffuse high → focal high → globally low), mirroring the three-way split in the `endo_hyperthyroid` flowchart.

**6th application of the umbrella/subset scoping rule.** The existing charts already own particular ground, so each new one was scoped to what it uniquely adds and cross-references the rest:
- `endo_hypothyroid` owns the **levothyroxine titration ladder**, so **`endo_hashimotos`** owns the **antibody and decision-to-treat pathway** instead — anti-TPO confirmation, then the three-way split into overt disease, subclinical disease (the TSH-above-10 rule versus a 6-month trial if symptomatic and under 65), and the euthyroid antibody-positive patient who needs only annual monitoring — ending on the **rapid painless enlargement → urgent lymphoma referral** red flag. It hands off to the Hypothyroidism entry for dosing.
- `endo_hyperthyroid` already three-way-splits on uptake and states "radioiodine first-line, antithyroid drugs do NOT cure nodular disease", so **`endo_toxic_mng`** does **not** re-tell the triage. It owns the **nodular goitre workup and definitive-treatment choice**: ultrasound and FNA of any dominant or cold nodule, the compression/retrosternal branch (CT or MRI plus flow-volume loop → thyroidectomy) versus the radioiodine branch, and the **Jod-Basedow** hazard from iodinated contrast and amiodarone, which nothing else in the app covered.
- **`endo_thyroid_storm`** had no owner at all and gets a full emergency chart, source **"Endocrine emergency"** to match Myxoedema coma. It is deliberately built around a **sequencing trap, exactly as the myxoedema coma chart is** — where that one turns on *hydrocortisone before thyroid hormone*, this one turns on **thionamide before iodine**.

**The drug sequence was verified on the web rather than recalled**, and the reason matters: **iodine given first supplies substrate and INCREASES thyroid hormone stores**, so Lugol's must follow the thionamide by **at least an hour**. Also captured: propylthiouracil is traditionally preferred because it additionally blocks peripheral T4-to-T3 conversion, though the evidence for superiority over carbimazole is not clear-cut; **hydrocortisone 300 mg then 100 mg 8-hourly**; **paracetamol not aspirin** for cooling, since aspirin displaces thyroid hormone from thyroid-binding globulin; and a **Burch-Wartofsky score of 45 or more** as highly suggestive. The entry states plainly that hormone levels **overlap with uncompensated thyrotoxicosis**, which is why the diagnosis is clinical and treatment must not wait for the assay.

Earlier: **ENGINE CHANGE — added an "⤢ Expand" button to every Tracker flowchart, opening a full-screen view with zoom and pan.** This is an `index.html` change, so **`index.html` must be re-uploaded or the button will not appear**. No content, ids or data files were touched.

**What was added.** The flowchart header in an expanded condition card now ends with an **⤢ Expand** button. Clicking it opens a full-screen modal containing the same diagram, with a header (condition name, `source` label, "hover a box for detail · drag to pan"), **zoom controls (− / percentage / + / Reset, clamped 50%–300% in 25% steps)** and a **✕ Close**. It closes on the ✕, a **backdrop click** or **Escape**, with `+`, `-` and `0` as zoom shortcuts, and the diagram can be **dragged to pan** when zoomed in. Hover tooltips work exactly as they do inline.

**How it was built (three components where there was one):**
- **`FlowDiagram`** — the existing render-plus-tooltip logic, extracted so the inline card and the modal can each own an instance. Each takes its own Mermaid id from `__flowSeq`, so two copies of the same diagram coexist without an id clash.
- **`FlowchartModal`** — the full-screen view, rendered through **`ReactDOM.createPortal` into `document.body`** so no ancestor `transform` or `overflow` can clip it. It locks `document.body.style.overflow` while open and restores the previous value on close.
- **`ManagementFlow`** — now a thin wrapper owning the header, the Expand button and the modal state. **The state had to live here** because the call site sits inside a `.map()` over conditions, where a hook cannot be added. The header markup (icon, source, hover hint) moved out of `TrackerView` into the component, so the call site shrank to four props.

**Two deliberate design points:**
- **Zoom does not re-render the diagram.** `applyFlowZoom` restyles the existing SVG's width from its viewBox width, cached on `dataset.baseW`. Re-running Mermaid on every zoom step would be slow and would drop the scroll position and the attached tooltip handlers.
- **The inline card is untouched.** It passes `zoom={null}`, which leaves Mermaid's own responsive `max-width` sizing alone, so the collapsed view looks and behaves exactly as before — verified by checking the inline SVG still carries Mermaid's `max-width` and no width of ours.

Verified in the preview: the JSX **compiles** (no console errors, `#root` populated, 19 specialties). Driving the real UI on `gi_femoral_hernia` and `cvs_mi`: the Expand button renders alongside the existing header on both; the modal opens by portal with the correct title and source and **all 13 / 15 nodes**; **zoom works** (933px → 1400px at 150%) and **clamps** at 300% (2799px) and 50% (467px); **Reset** returns to 100%; **hover tooltips still fire inside the modal** (checked by dispatching `mouseenter` on a node and reading the tooltip text); a **click on the panel does NOT close** (stopPropagation) while a **backdrop click, Escape and the ✕ all do**; `document.body.style.overflow` is restored and **no portal nodes are leaked** after close; and the inline diagram survives intact throughout. Screenshots confirmed the expanded view is markedly more readable, with the whole MI algorithm fitting on screen at 50%. **Re-upload `index.html`** (only app file changed). Earlier: **Added Inguinal hernia and Femoral hernia to the Gastro Tracker as two separate conditions**, both in the *Lower GI & colorectal* cluster. Neither existed — the only prior hernia entry anywhere in the Tracker was paediatric `paeds_cdh` (congenital diaphragmatic hernia), an unrelated entity, so these are not duplicates.

**Placement.** Inserted **between Diverticular disease and Peutz-Jeghers syndrome**, keeping the general-surgical entries together ahead of the neoplastic and anorectal runs. The cluster now reads Appendicitis → Diverticular disease → **Inguinal hernia → Femoral hernia** → Peutz-Jeghers → Colorectal cancer → Haemorrhoids → Anal fissure → Anal fistula. **Inguinal is placed first** even though the request named femoral first, because inguinal is far commoner and femoral is the dangerous exception that is best learned against it.

- **`gi_inguinal_hernia` Inguinal hernia** — seven-field + `complications` + a flowchart (source **"NICE CKS / British Hernia Society"**, 15 nodes). Content is built around the two discriminators: **above and medial to the pubic tubercle**, and indirect (through the deep ring, **lateral** to the inferior epigastric vessels, may reach the scrotum) versus direct (through the posterior wall **medial** to them, within Hesselbach's triangle). Notes that the deep-ring occlusion test is unreliable, so the two are reliably separated only at operation.
- **`gi_femoral_hernia` Femoral hernia** — seven-field + `complications` + a flowchart (source **"British Hernia Society"**, 13 nodes). Built around the mirror-image sign — **below and lateral to the pubic tubercle**, medial to the femoral pulse — and the anatomy that explains the behaviour: the femoral ring is bounded by the inguinal, pectineal (Cooper's) and lacunar ligaments and the femoral vein, so the neck is **narrow and rigid**, which is why it is often irreducible, frequently has no cough impulse, and strangulates early.

**The management divergence is the whole point of splitting them, and it was verified on the web rather than recalled:**
- **Watchful waiting is safe in MEN with an asymptomatic or minimally symptomatic INGUINAL hernia** — but roughly **a third cross over to surgery within 1.5 to 3 years and about 70% within 7 years**, so it is a deferral rather than an alternative.
- **ALL WOMEN with a groin hernia should be referred urgently**, because an apparently inguinal hernia may in fact be femoral. This is captured as an explicit branch in the inguinal flowchart and is easily missed.
- **EVERY femoral hernia is repaired urgently, symptomatic or not — watchful waiting is never appropriate.** The flowchart states this as a dedicated node.
- Repair approach: **open mesh (Lichtenstein)** for a primary unilateral inguinal hernia, **laparoscopic (TEP or TAPP)** for bilateral or recurrent; for femoral, **low (Lockwood)** electively versus **high (McEvedy)** where bowel resection may be needed, because the low approach gives poor abdominal access.

Both `complications` fields carry **Richter's hernia** — where only part of the bowel circumference is trapped, so the bowel **strangulates without obstructing** — and the femoral entry adds **Maydl's hernia**, where the strangulated loop lies back inside the abdomen and is missed unless the abdomen is opened.

**New topics:** `paediatric_asthma`, `acute_asthma_children`, `bronchiolitis_and_viral_wheeze`, `stridor_in_children`, `foreign_body_inhalation`, `paediatric_pneumonia_pertussis`, `common_cold_and_covid_children`, `cystic_fibrosis_management`, `paediatric_bronchiectasis`, `sleep_disordered_breathing_children`. Conditions were again grouped by clinical reasoning rather than alphabetically — the wheezy infant (bronchiolitis with viral episodic wheeze), the stridulous child (croup, epiglottitis, laryngomalacia in one differential table), and the two lower respiratory infections with specific antibiotic answers (pneumonia with pertussis).

**⚠️⚠️ THE BIG ONE: the guide's asthma section is entirely superseded, and this is the most consequential correction in the whole extraction project so far.** The guide follows **NICE CKS 2023**, reflecting the old **NG80** ladder — SABA alone, then add ICS, then LTRA, then LABA, then MART. That was **replaced in full on 27 November 2024** by the joint **BTS/NICE/SIGN guideline NG245**, which was itself **updated in November 2025**. Both dates are after the assistant's knowledge cutoff, so this was researched on the web rather than recalled. What is now written:
- **The overriding new rule, at every age: never prescribe a SABA without a concomitant ICS prescription.**
- **Two new terms** — **AIR** (anti-inflammatory reliever: ICS/formoterol taken *only* as needed) and **MART** (the same inhaler as maintenance *and* reliever).
- **Diagnosis in 5 to 16 year olds** is now an ordered objective pathway: **FeNO 35 ppb or more** is diagnostic; then bronchodilator reversibility (FEV1 up 12% or more); then PEF variability 20% or more over 2 weeks; then skin prick testing to house dust mite or IgE with **eosinophils above 0.5 x 10^9/L**; then bronchial challenge. **Under 5s cannot be tested** — treat with ICS and attempt objective tests at age 5.
- **Under 5s:** low-dose ICS as an 8–12 week trial → LTRA trial (with an explicit stop rule) → specialist. **No MART pathway exists for under-5s.**
- **5 to 11:** low-dose ICS + SABA → **MART preferred wherever the child can manage it** (low- then moderate-dose) → specialist; the conventional LTRA/ICS-LABA route is now **reserved for children who cannot manage MART**.
- **12 and over:** **as-needed AIR** → low-dose MART → moderate-dose MART → check FeNO and eosinophils (refer if either raised) → time-limited LTRA **or LAMA** trial → specialist.
- **Prescribing reality check from the Nov 2025 update:** the only inhaler licensed for MART in this age group is a **budesonide/formoterol DPI 100/6, licensed from age 6 to 11 — not 5**; any other ICS/formoterol for MART under 12 is **off-label**.
- Also captured: **LAMA is new**, digital inhalers are **not recommended for routine use**, FeNO monitoring is now considered at review in adults, and the **MHRA neuropsychiatric warning on montelukast** must be given.
The guide's **non-pharmacological** asthma content (action plan, immunisations, trigger avoidance including beta-blockers and NSAIDs, screening for anxiety and depression, peak flow meter, inhaler technique, review checklist) is unchanged by NG245 and was carried across in full.

**⚠️ Sibling topic now behind the guideline — flagged, NOT changed:** the adult `asthma_copd_compare` topic in `respiratory` (1025 chars) describes MART but predates NG245, so it lacks **AIR**, the **never-a-SABA-without-an-ICS** rule and the FeNO/eosinophil-guided step 4. It was left alone because this batch is paediatric, but it is thin and now out of date — **offer to rewrite it.**

**Other guideline points verified on the web:** **NICE NG9** bronchiolitis thresholds confirmed, including the deliberate asymmetry that **92% is the referral threshold but 90% the admission and oxygen threshold** from 6 weeks of age (92% under 6 weeks or with comorbidity).

**Gap-filled from knowledge:** the whole diagnostic and severity framing the guide omits — the effective-versus-ineffective cough distinction that drives the choking algorithm and why abdominal thrusts are forbidden in infants; the croup/epiglottitis/laryngomalacia differential table; that **stridor becoming quieter in croup is ominous**; the three phases of pertussis and apnoea as the infant presentation; that bronchiolitis gets **no bronchodilators, steroids, antibiotics or routine X-ray**; that most inhaled foreign bodies are **radiolucent** so a normal film does not exclude one, and the right main bronchus preference; childhood pneumonia organisms by age; infant botulism as the reason honey is banned under 1; congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens behind CF male infertility; Kartagener's link to the primary ciliary dyskinesia testing rule; and essentially the entire sleep-disordered-breathing topic — the guide gives only three lines of treatment, so the presentation (**hyperactive and inattentive rather than sleepy**, an ADHD mimic), the Down syndrome association and the investigation route were all added.

Earlier: **Notebook Batch 16b (Paediatric Cardiac Disease, Specialities Guide p87–93) — into `paediatrics`, which goes 11→18 topics.** Added **7 topics**, no augmentations, no id changed. Appended after the neonatal block and before `paediatric_hip_conditions` / `developmental_milestones`, keeping the section in the guide's own order.

The block's 15 conditions were grouped by **mechanism rather than alphabetically**, because that is how the lesions are actually reasoned about:
- **`congenital_heart_disease_overview` Congenital heart disease: the approach** — the master topic. The two framing questions (**is the child cyanosed?** left-to-right versus right-to-left; **is the lesion duct-dependent?**), a table splitting duct-dependent lesions into those dependent for **pulmonary flow, systemic flow and mixing**, the guide's cyanotic-newborn algorithm (**hyperoxia test**, the deliberately low **75–85% saturation target**, two cannulae or UVC/UAC, 10 mL/kg boluses to a maximum of 30, prostaglandin E1 and its full side-effect list led by **apnoea**), and **Eisenmenger syndrome**.
- **`acyanotic_shunt_lesions`** — ASD (three types, the closure indications including **Qp:Qs above 1.5**, transcatheter for secundum versus open surgery for primum), VSD (observation first-line, catheter or open closure, furosemide/captopril/digoxin, extra calories) and PDA (**indomethacin first-line**, ibuprofen, ligation or device closure), opening with a murmur table.
- **`obstructive_congenital_lesions`** — aortic stenosis (**balloon valvuloplasty first-line, because children's leaflets are not calcified** — the explicit contrast with adults), pulmonary stenosis, and coarctation (**98% distal to the left subclavian**, presenting **at 48 hours as the duct closes**, prostaglandin E1 then repair).
- **`cyanotic_heart_disease_lesions`** — tetralogy of Fallot with the full **tet spell** management and why knee-to-chest works, transposition (**balloon atrial septostomy**, arterial switch within 2 weeks, coronary transfer), and tricuspid atresia with the **three-stage BT shunt → Glenn → Fontan** table.
- **`paediatric_heart_failure`** — the guide's aims-of-management table (preload, contractility, afterload, oxygen delivery, nutrition, exercise) plus the infant presentation.
- **`paediatric_arrhythmia_syncope`** — SVT (vagal manoeuvres supine with legs raised, adenosine, then cardioversion/amiodarone 5 mg/kg/procainamide 15 mg/kg/flecainide 2 mg/kg; cardioversion first if unstable) and vasovagal syncope, ending with **the red flags that make syncope cardiac**.
- **`paediatric_endocarditis_rheumatic_fever`** — the paediatric specifics only, cross-referencing the adult `infective_endocarditis` and `rheumatic_fever` topics: the **0.43 per 100,000** incidence, pre-existing heart disease or an **indwelling central line** as the near-universal risk factor, the blood-culture timing rule, the empirical antibiotic table, and rheumatic fever's **naproxen/ibuprofen (not aspirin)** and **benzathine penicillin every 3–4 weeks**.

**⚠️ Guideline conflict found and resolved in favour of UK practice — the guide recommends endocarditis antibiotic prophylaxis in FOUR places** (infective endocarditis, VSD "prophylactic amoxicillin… during surgical procedures", pulmonary stenosis "before high-risk procedures", tricuspid atresia "needs antibiotic prophylaxis"). That reflects **European and American** guidance. **NICE CG64 does not agree**, and this was checked on the web rather than recalled: prophylaxis **"is not recommended *routinely*"** for people undergoing dental or other interventional procedures. The word "routinely" was added in **July 2016** and is load-bearing — it permits clinical judgement in individually high-risk patients — and in **October 2024** NICE amended recommendation 1.1.3 to signpost the **SDCEP (Scottish Dental Clinical Effectiveness Programme)** implementation advice that operationalises that decision. A 2024 meta-analysis did find prophylaxis reduced endocarditis after invasive dental procedures in high-risk individuals, but NICE decided not to update the evidence review, only to improve the signposting. The notebook therefore states the NICE position plainly, notes that any prophylaxis in a high-risk child is an individualised decision with the cardiology team, and keeps the emphasis NICE actually makes — **dental hygiene, regular dental review, avoiding piercings and tattoos**. The guide's four bare "give prophylaxis" statements were **not** reproduced as written.

**Gap-filled from knowledge (the guide gives management only, and defines almost nothing):** the acyanotic/cyanotic and duct-dependent framework itself; **Eisenmenger syndrome** and why it contraindicates closure — the guide never mentions it despite it being the whole reason shunts are closed; why an ASD murmur comes from the **pulmonary valve rather than the defect**, and the mechanism of **fixed splitting**; that a **smaller VSD makes a louder murmur**; the PDA machinery murmur and bounding pulses, and the rubella association; radiofemoral delay, rib notching, the 3 sign and the **Turner** and bicuspid-valve associations in coarctation; the **four components of tetralogy** and the boot-shaped heart (the guide names neither); the parallel-circulation explanation and **egg-on-a-string** film in transposition; the infant presentation of heart failure (feeding, sweating, **hepatomegaly not a raised JVP**); SVT rates above 220 in infants, **adenosine dosing at 100 then 200 then 300 micrograms/kg**, cardioversion at 1 then 2 J/kg, and **Wolff-Parkinson-White**; and the complete cardiac-syncope red flag list.

Earlier: **Notebook Batch 16a (Neonatal Medicine, Specialities Guide p78–87) — into the `paediatrics` section, which goes 2→11 topics.** Added **9 topics**, no augmentations, no id changed.

**⚠️ Batch 16 was too big and has been SPLIT into 16a–16i.** As written the roadmap gave Batch 16 as "Paediatrics, Specialities Guide p73–200" — **123 pages and roughly 200 conditions**, four to five times any previous batch. The Paediatrics section has its own contents page at **p75** and divides into 17 named blocks; the page boundaries were mapped and the blocks grouped into nine sub-batches of a size comparable to Batches 12–15. See the roadmap table below. **16a–16e are done. The paediatrics thread is PAUSED at 16f (Dermatology, p141–154) while the neurology thread (Batches 20–23) runs, and resumes there afterwards.**

**New topics.** The guide's neonatal block is a *management* guide — it gives treatment with almost no definition, presentation or investigation — so every topic needed substantial gap-filling to be usable, and the 25 listed conditions were grouped into nine coherent topics rather than written one-per-condition:
- **`neonatal_respiratory_distress` Respiratory distress in the newborn** — opens with the **differential table keyed on gestation, mode of delivery and chest X-ray** (the way the question is actually asked), then respiratory distress syndrome, transient tachypnoea, meconium aspiration, persistent pulmonary hypertension, pneumothorax and chronic lung disease of prematurity in full, including the 91–95% saturation target, the 60–80 breaths/minute feeding rule, the 4–6 hour antibiotic trigger, 100% oxygen for nitrogen washout, and caffeine citrate/palivizumab/home oxygen.
- **`neonatal_jaundice` Neonatal jaundice & haemolytic disease of the newborn** — the three timing bands (**under 24 hours is never physiological; over 14 days needs a split bilirubin**), transcutaneous versus serum bilirubin and which baby gets which, the treatment threshold graph, phototherapy → intensified phototherapy with IVIG → exchange transfusion, breast milk and conjugated jaundice, then **haemolytic disease of the newborn** with the anti-D regimens (1500 IU at 28 weeks, or 500 IU at 28 and 34), the exchange-transfusion criteria (rise above 8–10 micromol/L/hour, Hb below 100 g/L), IVIG above 8.5 micromol/L/hour, and **late anaemia at 4–6 weeks**.
- **`neonatal_sepsis_infection` Neonatal sepsis, group B streptococcus & hepatitis B** — early- versus late-onset, **benzylpenicillin and gentamicin per NICE NG195** with doses, the three GBS intrapartum-prophylaxis indications, listeria, the hepatitis B accelerated schedule and the three HBIG criteria, and **ophthalmia neonatorum** with its treatment table.
- **`congenital_infections_neonatal` Congenital infections: treating the newborn** — deliberately scoped to the **neonatal** management, cross-referencing `torch_infections` for the maternal side: CMV (**urine or saliva PCR within 2 weeks**, oral valganciclovir), the three maternal HSV scenarios as a table with **IV aciclovir never oral or topical**, and toxoplasmosis (pyrimethamine/sulfadiazine/folinic acid for a year, steroids for sight-threatening chorioretinitis).
- **`hypoxic_ischaemic_encephalopathy`** — precipitants, the supporting evidence of a hypoxic insult, **Sarnat staging as a three-stage table with outcomes**, and therapeutic hypothermia (33–34 degrees, 72 hours, rewarm over 6).
- **`neonatal_surgical_conditions`** — necrotising enterocolitis (including the 7-day feed hold and hourly aspirates), tracheo-oesophageal fistula and oesophageal atresia with the **Gross type A–E management table**, congenital diaphragmatic hernia, and cleft lip and palate.
- **`neonatal_hypoglycaemia`** — prevention, the asymptomatic pathway with the 1-hour recheck, and the symptomatic/below-2 pathway with **2 mL/kg then 3.6 mL/kg/hour of 10% glucose**, plus the hyperinsulinism options.
- **`down_syndrome_neonatal` Down syndrome & the chromosomal childhood syndromes** — diagnosis, the **45% cardiac / AVSD** and **10% transient abnormal myelopoiesis** figures, the surveillance list, the MDT, and an **11-row table of the chromosomal childhood syndromes** the guide merely lists.
- **`sudden_infant_death_syndrome` Sudden infant death syndrome & safer sleep** — risk factors, the full safer-sleep advice as counselling material, and the joint agency response to an unexpected infant death.

**Overlap handled rather than duplicated.** The neonatal block overlaps heavily with the O&G thread already written in Batches 5–10 and with `genetics`. `torch_infections` (CMV, HSV, toxoplasmosis, listeria, parvovirus, syphilis) and `pregnancy_uti_gbs` (GBS, hepatitis B) already own the **maternal** side, and `chromosomal_disorders` owns the genetics of trisomy 21, 13, 18, Turner and Klinefelter. The new topics therefore carry only the **neonatal** management and cross-reference the rest — which is where the genuinely new content is (valganciclovir, IV aciclovir, the toxoplasmosis triple therapy, the neonatal antibiotic choices).

**Guideline currency checked on the web, not recalled** — both cited guidelines are current, but one carries an update the guide predates:
- **NICE NG195** confirmed: **IV benzylpenicillin with gentamicin** remains first-choice empirical treatment for suspected early-onset infection (benzylpenicillin 25 mg/kg 12-hourly, gentamicin 5 mg/kg starting dose). A 2024 amendment concerns recurrent bacterial meningitis and does not affect this.
- **NICE CG98** (jaundice) thresholds are unchanged, but an **October 2023 update** added that **skin colour change is considerably harder to see in babies with darker skin**, so visual inspection alone is unsafe and the bilirubin should be measured. The guide has nothing on this and it is now stated explicitly in the topic. The same update restricted urine culture to cases with clinical suspicion of UTI, and a March 2023 safety statement was added about using the threshold tables.

**Gap-filled from knowledge (the guide gives management only):** the entire respiratory-distress differential table and every chest X-ray appearance; kernicterus and why unconjugated bilirubin is the dangerous fraction; biliary atresia as the reason prolonged jaundice needs a split bilirubin; early- versus late-onset sepsis organisms and the fact that a septic newborn is as likely to be hypothermic as febrile; the **48-hour gonococcal versus 5–14 day chlamydial** timing in ophthalmia neonatorum, and why cefotaxime is preferred over ceftriaxone in a neonate; the clinical features of congenital CMV, HSV and toxoplasmosis; Sarnat stage outcomes and the 6-hour window for cooling; pneumatosis intestinalis, Rigler's sign and short bowel syndrome in NEC; polyhydramnios, the unpassable NG tube, **type C at 85%** and the VACTERL association; pulmonary hypoplasia as the real problem in diaphragmatic hernia and why bag-and-mask ventilation is avoided; secretory otitis media after cleft palate; the at-risk groups and the neonatal interpretation of the hypoglycaemia screen; and the features of all eleven chromosomal syndromes.

Earlier: **Notebook Batch 15 (Calcium, stones, lipids, nutrition, metabolic disease and diabetes — Path Guide p112–116, p119–127 + the deferred p211 block).** Added **9 topics** across **four** sections and augmented **2 in place (ids kept, bookmarks safe)**. This is the first batch to spill outside its nominal target section, deliberately: the source block is Chemical Pathology, which is not all endocrine, and forcing renal stones or screening statistics into `endocrinology` would have put them where they will never be looked for.

**Into `endocrinology` (11→17)** — appended after `calcium_homeostasis` and `diabetes_overview` so the section still reads head-to-toe and then metabolic: pituitary → thyroid → adrenal → parathyroid (**+ hypocalcaemia**) → pancreas (**+ diabetic emergencies, hypoglycaemia**) → **lipids, obesity, inherited metabolic disease**.
- **`hypocalcaemia` Hypocalcaemia** — built around the guide's own discriminator, the **phosphate**: high phosphate points to CKD, hypoparathyroidism or pseudohypoparathyroidism; normal or low phosphate to osteomalacia, acute pancreatitis, overhydration or respiratory alkalosis. Carries the full symptom list (perioral paraesthesia, carpopedal spasm, Trousseau's and Chvostek's, impetigo herpetiformis, long QT), the **1.875 mmol/L** threshold for parenteral 10% calcium gluconate, oral calcium **away from meals**, and the alfacalcidol-in-CKD versus ergocalciferol/cholecalciferol split.
- **`diabetic_emergencies` Diabetic ketoacidosis & hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state** — the two sets of diagnostic criteria side by side, then each pathway in full: fluids before insulin, potassium in the second litre and never starting insulin below 3.5 mmol/L, **0.1 units/kg/h** for DKA versus **0.05** for HHS, the glucose-below-14 switch, resolution criteria, and the **3–8 mOsm/kg/h** osmolality ceiling. Consistent with the `endo_dka` / `endo_hhs` Tracker flowcharts.
- **`hypoglycaemia` Hypoglycaemia** — the guide's hyper-/hypoinsulinaemic split with the ketone sub-split, then the **C-peptide table** separating insulinoma from injected insulin from sulfonylurea, non-islet cell tumour hypoglycaemia (**"big IGF-2"**, everything low including ketones and free fatty acids), and the neonatal branch.
- **`lipid_disorders` Lipoprotein metabolism & the dyslipidaemias** — density order, PCSK9 and evolocumab, lipoprotein(a), the **14-row primary dyslipidaemia table** reproduced in full, familial hypercholesterolaemia clinically, and the statin/ezetimibe/inclisiran ladder.
- **`obesity_management` Obesity & weight management** — BMI **plus waist-to-height ratio**, the ethnicity adjustment, diet and activity, the four-drug table, and bariatric surgery.
- **`inherited_metabolic_disease` Newborn screening & inherited metabolic disease** — the blood spot programme and its five classic conditions with their screening assays, the explicit point that **it measures chemicals and is not a genetic test**, then the guide's **five-group** framework (toxin accumulation / reduced energy stores / large-molecule synthesis / large-molecule breakdown / mitochondrial) in full.
- **`calcium_homeostasis` augmented** — added the causes of primary (80% single adenoma, 15% hyperplasia, 0.5% carcinoma), secondary, tertiary and hypoparathyroidism; **Albright hereditary osteodystrophy**; a **vitamin D column** and a pseudohypoparathyroidism row in the biochemistry table; "thrones" in the mnemonic; and the guide's **routine-bloods algorithm** for hypercalcaemia (urea and albumin → cuffed specimen or dehydration; then phosphate; then ALP).
- **`diabetes_overview` augmented** — the four diagnostic thresholds and the symptomatic-versus-asymptomatic rule, **impaired glucose tolerance (7.8–11.0)** and **impaired fasting glucose (6.1–6.9)**, the T1-versus-T2 mechanism from p211 (**CD4 and CD8 mediated beta-cell destruction**), why polyuria and polydipsia happen, the other causes of hyperglycaemia, the **metabolic syndrome criteria**, and the macro-/microvascular complications table. **This closes the cross-batch deferral set in Batch 13 — the p211 diabetes block is now written.**

**Placed outside `endocrinology` on purpose:**
- **`renal_stones` Renal & ureteric stones → `renal` (5→6).** Urology, not endocrinology, and the section had no stones topic at all.
- **`nutrition_vitamins` Nutrition: vitamins, trace elements & deficiency states → `gastro` (20→21).** The specialty is literally *Gastroenterology & Nutrition*, and the guide's "specific conditions" list (Crohn's, coeliac, chronic liver disease, CKD, pancreatic insufficiency) is entirely GI.
- **`screening_test_statistics` Screening tests: sensitivity, specificity & predictive value → `public_health` (2→3).** The guide puts the statistics inside the metabolic chapter and notes they are more likely to be examined than the disorders themselves; they belong in *Public Health, Ethics & Evidence*, which was the second-thinnest section. Cross-referenced both ways with `inherited_metabolic_disease`.

**Guideline drift found — three of the guide's sections were materially out of date, and each was checked on the web rather than recalled:**
- **Obesity: the guide says "no medication has been safely proven to provide sustained weight loss."** This is now badly wrong. **NICE NG246 (published January 2025, updated January 2026)** replaced CG189 and six other guidelines, and recommends **tirzepatide** (TA1026, BMI 35+ with a weight-related comorbidity, prescribable in **primary care**), **semaglutide** (TA875) and **liraglutide** (TA664) in **specialist weight management services**, alongside orlistat. Also captured from NG246: assessment now needs **BMI plus waist-to-height ratio** (and do not measure waist above BMI 35), the **2.5 kg/m2 downward adjustment** for South Asian, Chinese, other Asian, Middle Eastern, Black African and African-Caribbean backgrounds, very-low-energy diets capped at **12 weeks** in specialist services only, and the person-first-language / ask-permission / avoid-diagnostic-overshadowing framing. The guide's orlistat and rimonabant material was kept and is still correct.
- **Lipids: CG181 has been replaced by NICE NG238** (December 2023). Written to NG238: QRISK3 for ages 25–84, **atorvastatin 20 mg** for primary prevention at 10-year risk of 10% or more, **atorvastatin 80 mg** for secondary prevention, lipids rechecked at **3 months** aiming for a **>40% fall in non-HDL cholesterol**, then ezetimibe, then **inclisiran** or a PCSK9 antibody, with secondary-prevention targets of LDL 2.0 or non-HDL 2.6 mmol/L. The guide had only "statins, side effects, ezetimibe rarely".
- **Renal stones: the guide's management is not NICE NG118 and conflates two different tables.** It gives a single size ladder ("≤5 mm conservative, 6–20 mm lithotripsy/ureteroscopy, >20 mm PCNL"). NG118 has **separate tables for renal and ureteric stones** — renal: under 10 mm offer SWL, 10–20 mm consider URS or SWL, over 20 mm including staghorn offer PCNL; ureteric: under 10 mm offer SWL, 10–20 mm offer URS. Also added from NG118 and absent from the guide: **NSAID by any route is first-line analgesia** (then IV paracetamol, then opioids) and **do not offer antispasmodics**; **alpha blockers for distal ureteric stones under 10 mm**; treatment **within 48 hours** if pain is ongoing or the stone is unlikely to pass; and the prevention specifics — **2.5–3 L/day**, fresh lemon juice, avoid carbonated drinks, salt under 6 g, **do not restrict calcium** (700–1200 mg/day), potassium citrate for recurrent >50% calcium oxalate stones, and thiazides only where there is **also hypercalciuria**. The guide's imaging recommendation was correct and is retained.

**Other guide errors corrected silently (recorded here per convention):**
- **p119 lipoprotein density order given as "Chylomicron < FFA < VLDL < LDL < IDL < HDL"** — wrong twice over: **free fatty acids are not a lipoprotein**, and **IDL sits between VLDL and LDL**, not between LDL and HDL. Written as chylomicron → VLDL → IDL → LDL → HDL.
- **p120 vitamin A deficiency given only as "colour blindness (retinitis pigmentosa)"** — the classic deficiency is **night blindness and xerophthalmia**; retinitis pigmentosa is a distinct inherited condition and was not carried across as a deficiency state.
- **p120 vitamin E deficiency listed as "anaemia/neuropathy/IHD"** — the anaemia is specifically **haemolytic**, and ataxia belongs with the neuropathy; ischaemic heart disease is an association rather than a deficiency state and is stated as such.
- **p116 "Cysteine" stones** — the stone is **cystine** (the disulphide dimer), not cysteine.
- **p116 "Triple phosphate (Mg, NH, PO)"** — magnesium **ammonium** phosphate; also added the point the guide omits, that struvite stones require a **urease-producing organism** such as *Proteus*, without which the staghorn cannot be explained or cured.
- **p124 "Group 3 … all dysmorphic"** and similar telegraphic labels were rewritten as full descriptions per house style.

**Gap-filled from knowledge (guide thin):** Whipple's triad and the autonomic-then-neuroglycopenic symptom sequence; taking the hypoglycaemia screen **before** giving glucose; the C-peptide table; euglycaemic DKA with SGLT2 inhibitors and continuing long-acting insulin through DKA; prophylactic anticoagulation in HHS; the magnesium rule in refractory hypocalcaemia and the low-normal calcium target in hypoparathyroidism; why respiratory alkalosis causes tetany with a normal total calcium; the obstructed infected kidney as the stone emergency; **SnNOut and SpPIn** and why predictive values move with prevalence; familial hypercholesterolaemia's clinical signs and the Simon Broome / Dutch Lipid Clinic criteria; Tangier disease's orange tonsils; the **Wolff-Chaikoff versus Jod-Basedow** distinction; and the unifying rule that anything impairing fat absorption causes A, D, E and K deficiency while terminal ileal disease is specific for B12.

**New topics:**
- **`pituitary_axis_function_tests` Pituitary axis & dynamic function testing** — the hypothalamic-hormone table (GHRH/GnRH/TRH/dopamine/CRH), the anterior-vs-posterior split, prolactin as the only hormone under **tonic inhibition** (which is what makes the stalk effect possible), then the whole **combined pituitary function test**: indications, the three contraindications (ischaemic heart disease, epilepsy, untreated hypothyroidism), side effects, the full procedure with doses (insulin 0.15 units/kg, TRH 200 mcg, LHRH 100 mcg; sampling at 0/30/60/90/120 min), and the three-part interpretation — insulin tolerance test (cortisol rise >170 to >500 nmol/L, GH >6 mcg/L), TRH test (TSH >5 mU/L, 30-min > 60-min), GnRH test (LH >10, FSH >2). Ends with gonadotrophin deficiency being diagnosed on **basal** levels, the clomifene/priming caveats, and the replacement list.
- **`pituitary_tumours` Pituitary tumours, prolactinoma & acromegaly** — micro (<10 mm) vs macroadenoma (>10 mm), mass effect (bitemporal hemianopia, cavernous sinus), the **stalk effect**, the three-band **prolactin elevation table** (<1000 / 1000–5000 / >5000 mIU/L), prolactinoma (dopamine agonist first, transsphenoidal second), non-functioning adenoma, and acromegaly in full (OGTT gold standard, IGF-1 for follow-up, high glucose/calcium/phosphate, the five-step management ladder, the follow-up list).
- **`thyroid_treatment` Treating hyperthyroidism & hypothyroidism** — symptom relief, carbimazole (titration vs block-and-replace, agranulocytosis warning), radioiodine, the **seven surgical indications**, the euthyroid-before-surgery rule, **thyroid storm**, and levothyroxine dosing/monitoring including the subclinical thresholds.
- **`thyroid_neoplasia` Thyroid nodules & thyroid cancer** — the malignancy risk features (solitary, solid, young, male, cold), the five-type table (papillary/follicular/medullary/anaplastic/lymphoma) with histology, spread, markers and management, thyroglobulin + TSH suppression on follow-up, and the MEN 1/2A/2B table.
- **`adrenal_insufficiency` Adrenal insufficiency & Addisonian crisis** — the primary/secondary split, the six-cause table, why pigmentation is primary-only, low Na + high K + hypoglycaemia, short Synacthen then **ACTH to separate primary from secondary**, hydrocortisone ± fludrocortisone, sick-day rules, and crisis management.
- **`cushings_syndrome` Cushing's syndrome** — CAPE, the ACTH-dependent/independent table with percentages, features, the first/second/third-line workup, **why the high-dose dexamethasone test was abandoned (20% false-positive rate) in favour of inferior petrosal sinus sampling**, and treatment.
- **`conn_phaeochromocytoma` Primary hyperaldosteronism & phaeochromocytoma** — the two surgically correctable adrenal causes of hypertension, aldosterone:renin ratio and adrenal vein sampling, spironolactone/eplerenone/amiloride vs adrenalectomy, then phaeo (triad, rule of tens, metanephrines, **ABC: alpha before beta**, and why beta-first is dangerous).

**`thyroid_overview` augmented in place** (renamed *Thyroid function tests & the causes of thyroid disease*; id unchanged) — now carries the reference ranges, the **eight-pattern TFT interpretation table**, subclinical disease and sick euthyroid syndrome, and two full cause tables: hypothyroidism (primary atrophic, Hashimoto's with Hurthle cells, iodine deficiency, post-treatment, drugs, Riedel's) and hyperthyroidism **classified by radionuclide uptake** (high = Graves'/toxic MNG/toxic adenoma; low = De Quervain's/postpartum/ectopic), since uptake is what determines whether antithyroid drugs will work.

**`siadh_di` also augmented in place** (id unchanged) — the completeness check found the guide's p105 ADH-failure block lists a **third** form of diabetes insipidus that the topic did not carry. Added **adipsic (hypothalamic) DI** (hypernatraemia without thirst; needs a prescribed fluid intake, not desmopressin alone), plus "around half of cranial cases are idiopathic" and chronic kidney disease among the nephrogenic causes.

**Guide errors corrected silently in the notebook (recorded here per convention):**
- **p105 "DA antagonists (cabergoline, bromocriptine)"** — these are dopamine **agonists**. Same error previously noted for cabergoline; corrected throughout.
- **p105 "Neurohypophysopathies → Anterior Hormones: ADH, Oxytocin"** — ADH and oxytocin are **posterior** pituitary hormones. Written correctly, with the anterior/posterior split made explicit.
- **p106 "Subclinical hyperthyroidism… may progress to primary hypothyroidism, especially if antiTPO positive"** — wrong condition: subclinical **hypo**thyroidism is what progresses (to overt hypothyroidism) and is what anti-TPO positivity predicts. The anti-TPO point was moved to the subclinical hypothyroidism paragraph.
- **p106 carbimazole framed as first-line with radioiodine as a fallback** — superseded by **NICE NG145**, which makes radioiodine the first-line *definitive* treatment for Graves' where drug remission is unlikely, and the treatment of choice for toxic nodular disease. Written to NG145 (consistent with the `endo_graves` / `endo_hyperthyroid` flowcharts realigned earlier the same day).
- **p110 Conn's syndrome given only "adrenal adenoma" as the cause** — **bilateral adrenal hyperplasia is the commoner cause**, and the distinction is the entire point of the workup (drugs vs surgery). Added, along with adrenal vein sampling, which the guide omits.
- **p110 "if >4cm consider surgical excision"** for Conn's — misleading as a treatment rule: adrenalectomy is for *unilateral* disease proven on adrenal vein sampling. The 4 cm figure is retained but stated correctly, as the threshold at which any adrenal mass raises suspicion of adrenocortical carcinoma.
- **p109 "inferior pituitary petrosal sinus sampling"** — the test is **inferior petrosal sinus sampling**.
- **p105 acromegaly "Octreotide… can't stop once started"** — not a real property of somatostatin analogues; dropped and the agent described correctly. **Pegvisomant** is specifically a GH **receptor** antagonist.
- **p105 prolactinoma ">6000"** vs the p104 table's ">5000 = macroprolactinoma"** — internally inconsistent; standardised on >5000 mIU/L.
- **p107 "Graves 40–60%, toxic MNG 30–50%"** — overlapping and unreliable ranges; written as "Graves' is the commonest cause" with only the toxic adenoma 5% figure retained.
- **p105 "Dipsogenic — failure/damage to hypothalamus and thirst drive, hypernatraemia without increased thirst"** — that describes **adipsic** DI; *dipsogenic* means primary polydipsia. Written as adipsic DI.

**What was wrong:** the old chart (7 nodes) presented **carbimazole as "first-line"** with radioiodine as a parallel "definitive" option. That predates NG145, which makes **radioiodine the first-line definitive treatment** for Graves' where antithyroid drugs are unlikely to achieve remission — so the Hyperthyroidism card was contradicting the adjacent Graves' card. The condition's **`treatment` field had the same stale ordering** ("Beta-blocker for symptoms; carbimazole; radioiodine or surgery") and was corrected too, since it sits on the same card.

**How it was rescoped (the umbrella/subset rule, 5th application):** rather than repeat the Graves' treatment ladder, the umbrella now owns the **cause-finding pathway**, which is genuinely its job because *the cause determines the treatment*. 7 → **14 nodes**: confirm biochemically (with a note on subclinical hyperthyroidism) → beta-blocker for symptoms in every cause → **TRAb** as first discriminator → if negative/equivocal, **radionuclide uptake scan** → three-way split on uptake pattern:
- **Diffuse high** → Graves' → *cross-references the Graves' entry* for the NG145 ladder rather than duplicating it.
- **Focal or patchy high** → toxic nodular goitre or toxic adenoma → **radioiodine first-line, because antithyroid drugs do NOT cure nodular disease** (it never remits, so thyrotoxicosis simply returns when the drug stops) → thyroidectomy if compression, suspected cancer or radioiodine unsuitable.
- **Globally low** → thyroiditis or exogenous thyroid hormone → **supportive only, NO antithyroid drugs** → cross-links the De Quervain's entry.
Ends with lifelong levothyroxine and TFT monitoring after definitive treatment. The useful detail from the old chart (propranolol, carbimazole agranulocytosis warning, propylthiouracil in pregnancy, thyroidectomy for compression) is retained in the tips, and thyroid storm is signposted as a separate emergency.

Neither existed.

- **`endo_graves` Graves' disease** — seven-field + a flowchart (source **"NICE NG145"**, 12 nodes). **The treatment pathway was verified on the web and differs from the classical teaching.** NG145 offers **radioactive iodine as FIRST-LINE definitive treatment** where antithyroid drugs are unlikely to achieve remission; only where remission IS likely (mild, uncomplicated) is there a shared-decision choice between carbimazole for 12–18 months (block-and-replace or titration) and radioiodine. Radioiodine is unsuitable in pregnancy or planned conception within 4–6 months, **active thyroid eye disease**, compression or suspected malignancy — then total thyroidectomy. Propylthiouracil if pregnant or conceiving within 6 months. Content carries the Graves-specific material the umbrella entry cannot: TSH receptor antibodies (positive ~95%), the **diffuse** increased uptake that separates it from thyroiditis (low) and toxic adenoma (focal), the extrathyroidal triad (eye disease, pretibial myxoedema, thyroid acropachy) and why it is unique to Graves, ~50% relapse after a drug course, carbimazole **agranulocytosis** warning plus the MHRA contraception and acute-pancreatitis advice, and smoking cessation as critical for eye disease.
- **`endo_de_quervain_thyroiditis` De Quervain's thyroiditis (subacute)** — seven-field + a flowchart (source "NICE CKS", 12 nodes). Built around the two discriminators: the **painful, tender** goitre (almost every other thyroid disorder is painless) and **globally LOW uptake** on scanning, the exact opposite of Graves. The management punchline is that **antithyroid drugs are NOT indicated**, because the thyrotoxicosis is release of preformed hormone from a damaged gland rather than overproduction — NSAIDs first-line, prednisolone for severe pain, beta-blocker for symptoms, then monitor through the **triphasic course** (thyrotoxic → euthyroid → hypothyroid → recovery in ~90%).

Seven-field layout + a flowchart (source **"NICE NG231"**, 14 nodes). **The surveillance intervals were verified on the web rather than recalled**, since NICE **NG231 (Barrett's oesophagus and stage 1 oesophageal adenocarcinoma, January 2023)** is the current UK guideline for this exact topic and the intervals are the kind of numeric detail worth checking. The chart branches on histology first, then on segment length and intestinal metaplasia:
- **No dysplasia** → **3 cm or longer: surveillance every 2 to 3 years**; **under 3 cm WITH intestinal metaplasia: every 3 to 5 years**; **under 3 cm WITHOUT intestinal metaplasia: consider not offering routine surveillance at all** (very low progression risk). NICE also advises tailoring frequency *within* these intervals using age, sex, family history of oesophageal cancer and smoking.
- **Indefinite for dysplasia** → optimise PPI and repeat endoscopy at 6 months (usually inflammation obscuring the histology).
- **Dysplasia or stage 1 adenocarcinoma** → specialist oesophago-gastric MDT → **endoscopic resection of any visible lesion** (also the most accurate depth staging) → **radiofrequency ablation of the residual segment** → post-treatment follow-up.

Content carries the high-yield diagnostic specifics: the **1 cm minimum** above the gastro-oesophageal junction, the **Prague C and M** classification, the **Seattle protocol** (quadrantic every 2 cm, every 1 cm if dysplasia suspected), the requirement for **two GI pathologists** to confirm dysplasia, the ~**0.3% per year** progression risk, that **Helicobacter pylori is inversely associated (protective)**, and the counter-intuitive point that reflux symptoms may **improve** as the less acid-sensitive columnar epithelium develops. **Deliberate scoping decision (the umbrella/subset trap again).** `gi_ugib` **already carries a variceal branch** in its flowchart (suspected varices → terlipressin + prophylactic antibiotics → band ligation → rebleed pathway), so this is the same overlap previously handled for `cvs_acs`/`cvs_mi`, bradycardia/heart block and cranial/nephrogenic DI. Rather than re-tell the generic acute upper-GI-bleed algorithm, the new flowchart owns the **variceal lifecycle** that UGIB cannot cover: **primary prophylaxis** (screening endoscopy in cirrhosis → non-selective beta-blocker or banding), variceal-specific haemostasis **split by site** (band ligation for oesophageal vs **cyanoacrylate glue for gastric** varices — banding does not work for gastric), **rescue** when endoscopy fails (**balloon tamponade as a BRIDGE only, max ~24 h**, then **TIPSS**, whose main complication is encephalopathy), and **secondary prophylaxis** (repeat banding to eradication + beta-blocker). The resuscitation node explicitly cross-references the Upper GI bleeding entry rather than duplicating it.

Content also carries the high-yield specifics: mortality ~15–20% per episode; the **restrictive transfusion target of about 70 g/L** because over-transfusion raises portal pressure and worsens bleeding; **terlipressin AND prophylactic antibiotics before endoscopy**, both of which improve survival; bleeding as a precipitant of encephalopathy (blood is a protein load); and that **isolated gastric varices should prompt a search for splenic vein thrombosis**. Earlier: **Added 2 Endocrinology Tracker conditions: Myxoedema coma and Hypocalcaemia.** Neither existed (the word "myxoedema" appeared nowhere in the tracker), so no duplicates.
- **`endo_myxoedema_coma` Myxoedema coma** — in the *Thyroid* cluster **directly after Hypothyroidism**, keeping the hypo- pair adjacent (cluster now reads Hypothyroidism → Myxoedema coma → Hyperthyroidism → Thyrotoxicosis → Thyroid eye disease). Seven-field layout + a flowchart (source "Endocrine emergency", 10 nodes). The pathway is built around the **sequencing trap**: **IV hydrocortisone 100 mg FIRST, before any thyroid hormone**, because coexisting adrenal insufficiency is common and giving thyroid hormone first accelerates cortisol metabolism and can precipitate an adrenal crisis. Then IV levothyroxine or liothyronine, **PASSIVE rewarming only** (active rewarming causes vasodilatation and circulatory collapse), ventilatory support for CO2 narcosis, cautious fluids, slow sodium correction, treat the precipitant, avoid all sedatives. Content notes that the name is a misnomer (frank coma is uncommon), mortality is 20–50%, and hypothermia masks the fever of the precipitating infection.
- **`endo_hypocalcaemia` Hypocalcaemia** — in the *Electrolyte disturbance* cluster after Hyponatraemia (cluster now Hyperkalaemia → Hyponatraemia → Hypocalcaemia). Seven-field layout + a flowchart (source "Society for Endocrinology", 11 nodes): severity split (tetany, seizures, long QT or corrected calcium below 1.9 → **IV 10% calcium gluconate 10–20 mL over 10 min** with cardiac monitoring, then infusion; otherwise oral calcium + vitamin D) → **check and correct MAGNESIUM** (hypocalcaemia is refractory until this is done) → **PTH as the discriminator**: low/inappropriately normal = hypoparathyroidism (alfacalcidol, aim low-normal calcium to avoid hypercalciuria and nephrocalcinosis) versus raised = vitamin D deficiency or CKD. Content stresses using the **albumin-corrected** calcium, Trousseau's and Chvostek's signs, digoxin potentiation, and the aetiology grouped by PTH.

| `endo_hhs` | JBDS HHS guideline **updated 2023**: insulin **0.05 units/kg/h** (half the DKA rate) and osmolality should fall only **3–8 mOsm/kg/h** | **Stale — under-specified** | Rebuilt 8→10 nodes, source "JBDS" |
| `renal_ckd` | **No 2025–26 NG203 update** (published Aug 2021). BUT **NICE TA877 (Mar 2023) recommends finerenone** and it was entirely absent | **Stale — missing third pillar** | Rebuilt 8→11 nodes, source "NICE NG203 / TA877" |

**What was actually fixed:**
- **`endo_t1dm`** — (1) **CGM emphasis corrected**: the chart said "self-monitor glucose 4+/day, offer CGM", but since the 2022 NG17 update NICE offers **rtCGM or isCGM to ALL adults** with type 1 diabetes, so CGM is the default and capillary testing the adjunct. (2) **Hybrid closed loop added** — NICE-recommended for HbA1c **58 mmol/mol or above OR disabling hypoglycaemia** despite a pump, rtCGM or isCGM; it was missing entirely. (3) The **Levemir discontinuation** is noted in the insulin tip so people on detemir get a planned switch.
- **`endo_dka`** — the chart started 10% glucose at a glucose below 14 but **never halved the insulin**. It now makes both changes at that single trigger: *"Add 10 percent glucose AND reduce insulin to 0.05 units/kg/h"*. Also made explicit: potassium **40 mmol/L** when K is 3.5–5.5, resolution as ketones under 0.6 **and** pH above 7.3, the overlap before stopping the infusion, euglycaemic DKA with SGLT2 inhibitors, and the severity markers for critical care.
- **`endo_hhs`** — the vague "start low-rate fixed insulin" is now **0.05 units/kg/h**, and a dedicated node carries the safety-critical **3–8 mOsm/kg/h** rate of osmolality fall (too-fast correction risks cerebral oedema and central pontine myelinolysis). Noted that JBDS deliberately permits a faster fall than the American guidance, that sodium **rises** initially as glucose falls, and the 24–72 hour correction window.
- **`renal_ckd`** — added the **finerenone** decision branch: stage 3–4 CKD with **ACR 3 mg/mmol or more** in type 2 diabetes, **eGFR 25 or above**, as add-on to **already-optimised ACEi/ARB AND SGLT2i**, with potassium monitoring (avoid starting if K above 5.0). Also added an explicit assessment node (eGFR + ACR classification), ACEi/ARB ACR thresholds, the "accept up to 25% eGFR fall" rule, and the nephrology referral criteria.
